Y,Y^{\prime},\dots,Y^{(r)}
,

\displaystyle z^{(r)}\

\displaystyle=\ Q\big(z,z^{\prime},\dots,z^{(r-1)}\big)+{\mathfrak{m}}^{-n}\big(y^{(r)}-P(y,y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r-1)})\big)

\displaystyle=\ Q\big(z,z^{\prime},\dots,z^{(r-1)}\big).

In
H\{Y\}
we have

(Y^{{\boldsymbol{j}}})_{\times{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}}=Y_{0}^{j_{0}}\cdots Y_{r}^{j_{r}}
for
{\boldsymbol{j}}=(j_{0},\dots,j_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}
.
Let

\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
; then
{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon}\in H
. We equip
H\{Y\}
with the gaussian extension of the valuation of
H
; see [ADH, 4.5]. Then
{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-n}(Y^{{\boldsymbol{j}}})_{\times{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon}}
for
{{\boldsymbol{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}\setminus\{0\}}
, by [ADH, 6.1.4].
Take
Q_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]

for
\lVert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rVert<r
such that

Q\ =\ \sum_{\lVert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rVert<r}Q_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}},\qquad(Q_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\neq 0\text{ for only finitely many }{\boldsymbol{i}}).

Together with
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\preccurlyeq\eta
for all
{\boldsymbol{i}}
and
P(0)\preccurlyeq\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
, the remarks above
yield
Q_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\preccurlyeq\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon}
for all
{\boldsymbol{i}}
.
By Theorem
2.5
applied to
P
,
y
,
\eta
replaced by
Q
,
z
,
\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon}
, we now obtain
z^{(j)}\preccurlyeq(\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon})^{j}
, with
\prec
in place of
\preccurlyeq
if
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
and
P(0)\prec\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
.
Using Lemma
2.6
with
\eta\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{-\varepsilon}
in place of
\eta
finishes the proof of Proposition
2.4
. ∎

The following immediate consequence of Proposition
2.4
is used in Section
4
. (The case
{\mathfrak{m}}=1
is due to Esclangon
[
38
]
.)

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
satisfy

y^{(r)}+f_{1}y^{(r-1)}+\cdots+f_{r}y\ =\ 0,\qquad f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\preccurlyeq\eta,\quad y\ \preccurlyeq\ {\mathfrak{m}}^{n}.

Then
y^{(j)}\preccurlyeq\eta^{j}{\mathfrak{m}}^{n-j(1+\varepsilon)}
for
j=0,\dots,r
and all
\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
, with
\prec
in place of
\preccurlyeq
if
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
.

3.
Hardy Fields and Uniform Distribution

Section
4
gives an analytic description of the universal exponential extension
{\operatorname{U}}
of the algebraic closure
K
of a Liouville closed Hardy field extending
{\mathbb{R}}
. The elements of
{\operatorname{U}}
are exponential sums with coefficients and exponents in
K
.
To extract asymptotic information about the summands in such a sum we refine here results of Boshernitzan
[
25
]
about uniform distribution mod
1
for
functions whose germs are in a Hardy field.
Our reference for uniform distribution mod
1
is
[
58
, Ch. 1, §9]
.
We also need some facts about
trigonometric polynomials, almost periodic functions, and their mean values. These are treated in
[
16
,
31
]
mainly in the one-variable case; adaptations to the multivariable case, required here, generally are straightforward.

Throughout this section we assume
n\geqslant 1
.
We equip

{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with its usual Lebesgue measure
\mu_{n}
, and
measurable
means measurable with respect to
\mu_{n}
.
For vectors
r=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
and
s=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we let
{r\cdot s}:={r_{1}s_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}s_{n}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be the usual dot product of
r
and
s
. We also set

rs:=(r_{1}s_{1},\dots,r_{n}s_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, not to be confused with
r\cdot s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover, we let
{v,w\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}}
be complex-valued functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and let
s=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
range over
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and
T
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
.

We set
|s|=|s|_{\infty}:=\max\big\{\lvert s_{1}\rvert,\dots,\lvert s_{n}\rvert\big\}
and
\lVert w\rVert:=\sup_{s}\,\lvert w(s)\rvert\in[0,+\infty]
.
We shall also have occasion to consider various functions
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
obtained from
w
:
\overline{w}
,
\lvert w\rvert
, as well as
w_{+r}
and
w_{\times r}
(for
r\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
), defined
by

\overline{w}(s)\ :=\ \overline{w(s)},\quad\lvert w\rvert(s)\ :=\ \lvert w(s)\rvert,\quad w_{+r}(s)\ :=\ w(r+s),\quad w_{\times r}(s)\ :=\ w(rs).

Almost periodic functions

Let
\alpha
range over
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Call
w
a
trigonometric polynomial
if there are
w_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}
, with
w_{\alpha}\neq 0
for only finitely many
\alpha
,
such that

(3.1)

w(s)\ =\ \sum_{\alpha}w_{\alpha}\operatorname{e}^{(\alpha\cdot s)\imag}\qquad\text{for all $s$.}

The coefficients
w_{\alpha}
in (
3.1
) are uniquely determined by
w
. (See Corollary
3.6
below.)
If
w
is a trigonometric polynomial, then
\overline{w}

is a trigonometric polynomial, and for
r\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, so are
w_{+r}
and
w_{\times r}
.
The trigonometric polynomials form a subalgebra of the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra of
uniformly continuous bounded functions
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
.
The latter is a Banach algebra
with respect to
\|\cdot\|
, and the elements of the closure of
its subalgebra of trigonometric polynomials with respect to this norm
are the
almost periodic
functions (in the sense of Bohr), which therefore form a Banach subalgebra.
In particular, if
v
,
w
are almost periodic, so are

v+w
and
vw
. Moreover, if
w
is almost periodic, then so are
\overline{w}
and
w_{+r}
,
w_{\times r}
for
r\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

We say that
v
is
1
-periodic
if
v_{+k}=v
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}
.
If
v
is continuous and
1
-periodic, then
v
is almost periodic: indeed, by “Stone-Weierstrass” there is for every
\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
a
1
-periodic trigonometric polynomial
w
with
{\lVert v-w\rVert<\varepsilon}
.
(See
[
33
, (7.4.2)]
for the case
n=1
.)

Mean value

The function
w
is said to have a
mean value
if
w
is bounded and measurable, and the limit

(3.2)

\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T^{n}}\int_{[0,T]^{n}}w(s)\,ds

exists (in
\mathbb{C}
); in that case we
call the quantity (
3.2
) the
mean value
of
w
and denote it by
M(w)
.
One verifies easily that if
v
and
w
have a mean value, then so do the functions
v+w
,
cw
(
c\in\mathbb{C}
),
and
\overline{w}
, with

M(v+w)\ =\ M(v)+M(w),\quad M(cw)\ =\ cM(w),\quad\text{ and }\quad M(\overline{w})\ =\ \overline{M(w)}.

If
w
has a mean value and
w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, then
M(w)\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
d\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then
w
has a mean value iff
w_{+d}
has a mean value,
in which case
M(w)=M(w_{+d})
.

Proof.

It suffices to treat the case
d=(d_{1},0,\dots,0)
,
d_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
.
For
T>d_{1}
we have

\left|\int_{[0,T]^{n}}w_{+d}(s)\,ds-\int_{[0,T]^{n}}w(s)\,ds\right|\ =\ \\
\left|\int_{[T,d_{1}+T]\times[0,T]^{n-1}}w(s)\,ds-\int_{[0,d_{1}]\times[0,T]^{n-1}}w(s)\,ds\right|\ \leqslant\ 2d_{1}\lVert w\rVert T^{n-1},

and this yields the claim.
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

Suppose
w
has a mean value and
T_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
.
If
w(s)=0
for all
s\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant})^{n}
with
\lvert s\rvert\geqslant T_{0}
, then
M(w)=0
.
If
w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
and
w(s)\geqslant 0
for all
s\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant})^{n}
with
\lvert s\rvert\geqslant T_{0}
, then
M(w)\geqslant 0
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
w
has a mean value and
w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

\liminf_{\lvert s\rvert\to\infty}w(s)\ \leqslant\ M(w)\ \leqslant\ \limsup_{\lvert s\rvert\to\infty}w(s)\qquad\text{ with $s$ ranging over $({\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant})^{n}$}.

Proof.

Assume
L:=\limsup_{\lvert s\rvert\to\infty}w(s)<M(w)
. Let
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{2}\big(M(w)-L\big)
, and
take
T_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
such that
w(s)\leqslant M(w)-\varepsilon
for all
s
with
\lvert s\rvert\geqslant T_{0}
.
The previous corollary then yields
M(w)\leqslant M(w)-\varepsilon
, a contradiction.
This shows the second inequality; the first inequality is proved in a similar way.
∎

The following is routine:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(v_{m})
be a sequence of bounded measurable functions
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
with a mean value, such that
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\lVert v_{m}-w\rVert=0
, and let
w
be bounded and measurable. Then

w
has a mean value, and
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}M(v_{m})=M(w)
.

If
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and

w(s)=\operatorname{e}^{\imag(\alpha\cdot s)}
for all
s
, then
w
has a mean value, with
M(w)=1
if
\alpha=0
and
M(w)=0
otherwise.
Together with Lemma
3.4
, this yields the well-known fact that if
w
is almost periodic, then
w
has a mean value. (See
[
31
, Theorem 1.12]
for the case
n=1
.)
Moreover, using Lemma
3.4
it is also easy to show that if
w
is almost periodic and
r\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{n}
, then
the almost periodic function
w_{\times r}
has the same mean value as
w
.
If
w
is continuous and
1
-periodic, then
w
has a mean value, namely
M(w)=\int_{[0,1]^{n}}w(s)\,ds
.
For a proof of the following result in the case
n=1
, see
[
31
, Theorem 1.19]
:

Proposition 3.5

(Bohr)
.

If
w
is almost periodic,

w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
, and
M(w)=0
, then
w=0
.

By Proposition
3.5
, the map
(v,w)\mapsto\langle v,w\rangle:=M(v\overline{w})
is a positive definite hermitian form on the
\mathbb{C}
-linear space of almost
periodic functions
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
.
For a trigonometric polynomial
w
as in (
3.1
) we have
w_{\alpha}=\langle w,\operatorname{e}^{(\alpha\cdot s)\imag}\rangle
, and thus:

Corollary 3.6
.

For
w
as in (
3.1
),
if
w=0
, then
w_{\alpha}=0
for all
\alpha
.

Uniform distribution

In this subsection
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are measurable, and
f:=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Theorem 3.7

(Weyl)
.

The following conditions on
f
are equivalent:

(i)

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\imag(k\cdot f(t))}\,dt\ =\ 0
for all
k\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{n})^{\neq}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}(w\circ f)(t)\,dt\ =\ \int_{[0,1]^{n}}w(s)\,ds
for every continuous
1
-periodic
w
;

(iii)

for every continuous
1
-periodic

w
, the function
{w\circ f}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{C}
has mean value
M(w\circ f)=M(w)
.

We say that

f
is
uniformly distributed
\operatorname{mod}\,1
(abbreviated: u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1
)
if
f
satisfies condition (i) in Theorem
3.7
.
This is not the usual definition
but is equivalent to it by
[
58
, Theorem 9.9]
.
The latter, in combination with
[
58
, Exercise 9.26]

also yields the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) in Theorem
3.7
.
For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i), apply (ii) to the
1
-periodic trigonometric polynomial
w
given by
w(s)=\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\imag(k\cdot s)}
.
The equivalence (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) is clear by earlier remarks.

In the next lemma we consider a strengthening of u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

set
\alpha f:=(\alpha_{1}f_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}f_{n})\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The following conditions on
f
are equivalent:

(i)

\alpha f
is u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1
for all
\alpha\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{n}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\imag(\beta\cdot f(t))}\,dt\ =\ 0
for all
\beta\in({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{\neq}
;

(iii)

for every almost periodic

w
,
w\circ f
has mean value
M(w\circ f)=M(w)
.

Proof.

Assume (i); let
\beta\in({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{\neq}
. For
i=1,\dots,n
set
\alpha_{i}:=1
,
k_{i}:=0
if
\beta_{i}=0
and
\alpha_{i}:=\beta_{i}
,
k_{i}:=1
if
\beta_{i}\neq 0
. Then
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{n})^{\neq}
,

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
is in
({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{n}
, and
\beta\cdot f(t)=k\cdot(\alpha f)(t)
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Now (ii) follows from the above definition of “u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1
” applied to
\alpha f
in place of
f
.
For (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii), assume (ii). Then each trigonometric polynomial
v
gives a function
v\circ f
with mean
value
{M(v\circ f)}=M(v)
.
Let
w
be almost periodic, and
take a sequence
(v_{m})
of trigonometric
polynomials
{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}}
such that
{\lVert v_{m}-w\rVert\to 0}
as
m\to\infty
.
So
M(v_{m})\to M(w)
as
m\to\infty
, by Lemma
3.4
.
Also
\lVert(v_{m}\circ f)-(w\circ f)\rVert\to 0
as
m\to\infty
,
hence by Lemma
3.4
again,

w\circ f
has a mean value and

M(v_{m})=M(v_{m}\circ f)\to M(w\circ f)
as
m\to\infty
. Therefore
M(w\circ f)=M(w)
.
Finally, assume (iii), and let
\alpha\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})^{n}
; to show that
\alpha f
is
u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1
we verify that condition (iii) in Theorem
3.7

holds for
\alpha f
in place of
f
. Thus suppose
w
is continuous and
1
-periodic. By (iii) applied to the almost periodic function
w_{\times\alpha}
in place of
w
, the function
w_{\times\alpha}\circ f=w\circ(\alpha f)
has a mean value and
{M(w_{\times\alpha}\circ f)}=M(w_{\times\alpha})
;
now use that
M(w_{\times\alpha})=M(w)
by a remark after Lemma
3.4
.
∎

We say that
f
is
uniformly distributed
(abbreviated: u.d.) if it satisfies one of the equivalent conditions in Lemma
3.8
.

Corollary 3.9
.

Suppose
w
is almost periodic,
w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
, and
f
is u.d. Then

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}w\big(f(t)\big)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ w=0.

Proof.

Lemmas
3.3
and
3.8
give
0\leqslant M(w)=M(w\circ f)\leqslant\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}w\big(f(t)\big)
. Now use Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Application to Hardy fields

In this subsection
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are continuous, their germs, denoted also by
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
, lie in a common Hardy field, and as above
f:=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Theorem 3.10

(Boshernitzan
[
25
, Theorem 1.12]
)
.

\text{$f$ is u.d.~$\operatorname{mod}\,1$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{$k_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}f_{n}\succ\log x$ for all $(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{n})^{\neq}$.}

For example, given
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the map
t\mapsto(\lambda_{1}t,\dots,\lambda_{n}t)\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is u.d.
\operatorname{mod}\,1

iff
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linearly independent (Weyl
[
89
]
).

Corollary 3.11
.

We have the following equivalence:

\text{$f$ is u.d.}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{$\beta_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{n}f_{n}\succ\log x$ for all $(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{\neq}$.}

In particular, if
\log x\prec f_{1}\prec\cdots\prec f_{n}
, then
f
is u.d.

Proof.

Let
\beta=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{\neq}
and define
\alpha_{i}
,
k_{i}
as in the proof of (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) in Lemma
3.8
.
Then
{\beta_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{n}f_{n}}=k_{1}(\alpha_{1}f_{1})+\cdots+k_{n}(\alpha_{n}f_{n})
, and the germs
\alpha_{1}f_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}f_{n}
lie in a common Hardy field by Proposition
2.1
. Using this observation and Theorem
3.10
yields the forward direction. The backward direction is also an easy consequence of Theorem
3.10
.
∎

We now get to the result that we actually need in Section
4
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Suppose
w
is almost periodic,
w({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
,
1\prec f_{1}\prec\cdots\prec f_{n}
, and

\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}w\big(f(t)\big)=0
. Then
w=0
.

Proof.

We first arrange
f_{1}>{\mathbb{R}}
, replacing
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
and
w
by
-f_{1},\dots,-f_{n}
and the function
s\mapsto w(-s)\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
, if
f_{1}<{\mathbb{R}}
.
Pick
a
such that the restriction of
f_{1}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant a}
is strictly increasing, set
b:=f_{1}(a)
, and let

f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant b}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the compositional inverse of this restriction.
Set
g_{j}(t):=(f_{j}\circ f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}})(t)
for
t\geqslant b
and
j=1,\dots,n

and consider the map

g\ =\ (g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\ =\ f\circ f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}}\ :\ {\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant b}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

The germs of
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
, denoted by the same symbols,
lie in a common Hardy field (see for example
[
9
, Section 4]
) and satisfy
x=g_{1}\prec g_{2}\prec\cdots\prec g_{n}
.
Now
f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}}
is strictly increasing and moreover
f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}}(t)\to+\infty
as
t\to+\infty
, so

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}w\big(f(t)\big)\ =\ \limsup_{t\to+\infty}w\big(f\big(f_{1}^{\operatorname{inv}}(t)\big)\big)\ =\ \limsup_{t\to+\infty}w\big(g(t)\big)\ =\ 0.

Thus replacing
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
by continuous functions
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with the same
germs as
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
, we arrange
x=f_{1}\prec f_{2}\prec\cdots\prec f_{n}
.
Then
f
is u.d. by Corollary
3.11
. Now use Corollary
3.9
.
∎

4.
Universal Exponential Extensions of Hardy Fields

In this section
H\supseteq{\mathbb{R}}
is a Liouville closed Hardy field and
K:=H[\imag]
. So
K
is an algebraically closed
\operatorname{d}
-valued field with constant field
{\mathbb{C}}
.
In order to give an analytic
description of the universal differential extension
{\operatorname{U}}={\operatorname{U}}_{K}
of
K

we consider the differential ring extension
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
of
K

with ring of constants
{\mathbb{C}}
. For any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have a
g\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]

with
g^{\prime}=f
, and then
u=\operatorname{e}^{g}\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]^{\times}
satisfies

u^{\dagger}=f
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
is purely imaginary, that is,

f\in\imag\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
. Then there is a
u\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]^{\times}

such that
u^{\dagger}=f
and
|u|=1
.

Proof.

Taking
g\in\imag\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
with
g^{\prime}=f
, the resulting

u=\operatorname{e}^{g}
works.
∎

We define the subgroup
\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
of
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]^{\times}
by

\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}\ :=\ \big\{\!\operatorname{e}^{h\imag}:\ h\in H\big\}\ =\ \big\{u\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]^{\times}:\ |u|=1,\ u^{\dagger}\in H\imag\big\}.

Then

(\operatorname{e}^{H\imag})^{\dagger}=H\imag
by Lemma
4.1
, so
(H^{\times}\cdot\operatorname{e}^{H\imag})^{\dagger}=K

and thus
K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
is an exponential extension of
K
with the same ring of constants
\mathbb{C}
as
K
.
Fix a complement
\Lambda\subseteq H\imag
of the subspace
K^{\dagger}
of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear space
K
, and
let
\lambda
range over
\Lambda
.
The differential
K
-algebras
{\operatorname{U}}:=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big]
and

K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
are isomorphic by Corollary
1.3
, but we need something better:

Lemma 4.2
.

There is an isomorphism
{\operatorname{U}}\to K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]

of differential
K
-algebras that maps
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
into
\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
.

Proof.

We have a short exact sequence of commutative groups

1\to S\xrightarrow{\ \subseteq\ }\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}\xrightarrow{\ \ell\ }H\imag\to 0,

where
S=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}:\,|z|=1\big\}
and

\ell(u):=u^{\dagger}
for
u\in\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
. Since the subgroup
S
of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is divisible, this sequence splits: we have a group embedding

e\colon H\imag\to\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
such that
e(b)^{\dagger}=b
for all
b\in H\imag
. Then
the group embedding

\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\mapsto e(\lambda)\ :\ \operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\to\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}

extends uniquely to a
K
-algebra morphism
{\operatorname{U}}\to K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
. Since

\operatorname{e}(\lambda)^{\dagger}=\lambda=e(\lambda)^{\dagger}
for all
\lambda
, this is a
differential
K
-algebra morphism, and even an isomorphism by
Lemma
1.2
applied to
R=K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
.
∎

Complex conjugation
f+g\imag\mapsto\overline{f+g\imag}=f-g\imag
(
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
) is an automorphism of the differential ring
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
over
H
and maps

K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
onto itself, sending each
{u\in\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}}
to
u^{-1}
. Thus any isomorphism

\iota\colon{\operatorname{U}}\to K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
of differential
K
-algebras with
\iota\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)\subseteq\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
as in Lemma
4.2
also satisfies

\iota(\overline{f})=\overline{\iota(f)}
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
,
where
f\mapsto\overline{f}
is the ring automorphism of
{\operatorname{U}}
which extends complex conjugation on
K=H[\imag]

and satisfies
\overline{\operatorname{e}(\lambda)}=\operatorname{e}({-\lambda})
for all
\lambda
;
see
[
8
, Section 2.2, subsection “The real case”]
.) Hence by
[
8
, Lemma 2.2.14, Corollary 2.2.17]
,
given such an isomorphism
\iota
, any differential
K
-algebra isomorphism as in Lemma
4.2
equals
\iota\circ\sigma_{\chi}
for a unique character
\chi\colon\Lambda\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
with
\lvert\chi(\lambda)\rvert=1
for all
\lambda
, where
\sigma_{\chi}
is the differential
K
-algebra
automorphism of
{\operatorname{U}}
with
\sigma_{\chi}(\operatorname{e}(\lambda))=\chi(\lambda)\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda
.
Fix such an isomorphism
\iota
and identify
{\operatorname{U}}
with its image
K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
via
\iota
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
. Then the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
has a basis

f_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag},\dots,f_{d}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{d}\imag}\quad\text{ where $f_{j}\in K^{\times}$, $\phi_{j}\in H$ {(}$j=1,\dots,d${)}.}

If
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, then we can choose the
f_{j}
,
\phi_{j}
such that in addition, for each
j
, we have
\phi_{j}=0
or
\phi_{j}\succ 1
.

Proof.

By the remarks before (
1.2
),
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
has a basis contained in
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}=K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
. Using
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\subseteq\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}
, this yields the first part. For the second part, use that if
{\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}}
and
\phi\in H
,
\phi\preccurlyeq 1
, then
\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag}\in K^{\times}
by
[
9
, Proposition 6.11]
.
∎

Remark
.

Let
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
, and suppose
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
. Then one can choose the
f_{j}
,
\phi_{j}
as in Lemma
4.3
such that also for all
j\neq k
,
either

\phi_{j}-\phi_{k}\succ 1
, or
{\phi_{j}=\phi_{k}}
and
vf_{j}\neq vf_{k}
in
v(K^{\times})
. For such
f_{j}
,
\phi_{j}
we have
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(A)\supseteq\{vf_{1},\dots,vf_{d}\}
. These statements are proved in
[
10
]
and not used later in this paper, but explain how
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(A)
helps
to locate the
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
with
A(y)=0
.

We have the asymptotic relations

\preccurlyeq_{\operatorname{g}}
and
\prec_{\operatorname{g}}

on
{\operatorname{U}}
coming from the gaussian extension
v_{\operatorname{g}}
of the valuation on
K
. (See Section
1
.) But we also have the asymptotic relations induced on
{\operatorname{U}}=K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]

by the relations
\preccurlyeq
and
\prec
defined on
\mathcal{C}[\imag]
. It is clear that for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
:

\displaystyle f\preccurlyeq_{\operatorname{g}}1

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f\preccurlyeq 1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{for some $n$ we have $\lvert f(t)\rvert\leqslant n$ eventually,}

\displaystyle f\prec_{\operatorname{g}}1

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f\prec 1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=0.

As a tool for later use we derive a converse of the implication

f\prec_{\operatorname{g}}1\Rightarrow f\prec 1
: Lemma
4.7
below, where we assume in addition
that
{\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}}
and
\Lambda
is an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear subspace
of
K
.
This requires the material from Section
3
and some considerations about exponential sums treated in the next subsection.

Exponential sums over Hardy fields

In this subsection
n\geqslant 1
.
In the next lemma,
f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\in H^{m}
where
m\geqslant 1
and

1\prec f_{1}\prec\cdots\prec f_{m}
. (In that lemma it doesn’t matter which
functions we use to represent the germs
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
.)
For
r=(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
we set
r\cdot f:=r_{1}f_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}f_{m}\in H
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
r^{1},\dots,r^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
be distinct and
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Then

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\left|c_{1}\operatorname{e}^{(r^{1}\cdot f)(t)\imag}+\cdots+c_{n}\operatorname{e}^{(r^{n}\cdot f)(t)\imag}\right|\ >\ 0.

Proof.

Consider the trigonometric polynomial
w\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
given by

w(s)\ :=\ \big|c_{1}\operatorname{e}^{(r^{1}\cdot s)\imag}+\cdots+c_{n}\operatorname{e}^{(r^{n}\cdot s)\imag}\big|^{2}.

By Corollary
3.6
we have
w(s)>0
for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
Taking continuous representatives
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
, to be denoted also by
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
, the lemma now follows from Proposition
3.12
.
∎

Next, let
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\in H
be distinct such that

({\mathbb{R}}h_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbb{R}}h_{n})\cap\operatorname{I}(H)=\{0\}
. Since
H
is Liouville closed we have

\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}\in H
such that
\phi_{1}^{\prime}=h_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}^{\prime}=h_{n}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Then for
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}
as above,

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\left|c_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}(t)\imag}+\cdots+c_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}(t)\imag}\right|\ >\ 0.

Proof.

The case
n=1
is trivial, so let
n\geqslant 2
. Then
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}
are not all in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Set
V:={\mathbb{R}}+{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{n}\subseteq H
, so

\der V={\mathbb{R}}h_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbb{R}}h_{n}
.
We claim that
{V\cap\smallo_{H}=\{0\}}
.
To see this, let
\phi\in V\cap\smallo_{H}
; then
\phi^{\prime}\in\der(V)\cap\operatorname{I}(H)=\{0\}
and hence
\phi\in{\mathbb{R}}\cap\smallo_{H}=\{0\}
,
proving the claim.
Now
H
is a Hahn space over
{\mathbb{R}}
by [ADH, p. 109], so by [ADH, 2.3.13] we have
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\in V
(
1\leqslant m\leqslant n
) such that
V={\mathbb{R}}+{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbb{R}}f_{m}
and
1\prec f_{1}\prec\cdots\prec f_{m}
. For
j=1,\dots,n
,
k=1,\dots,m
,
take
t_{j},r_{jk}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\phi_{j}=t_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}r_{jk}f_{k}

and set
r^{j}:=(r_{j1},\dots,r_{jm})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
Since
\phi_{j_{1}}-\phi_{j_{2}}\notin{\mathbb{R}}
for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
, we have

r^{j_{1}}\neq r^{j_{2}}
for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
.
It remains to apply Lemma
4.4
to
c_{1}\operatorname{e}^{t_{1}\imag},\dots,c_{n}\operatorname{e}^{t_{n}\imag}
in place of
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in K
and set
f:=f_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+f_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
, and suppose
f\prec 1
. Then
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\prec 1
.

Proof.

We may assume
0\neq f_{1}\preccurlyeq\cdots\preccurlyeq f_{n}
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that
{f_{n}\succcurlyeq 1}
, and
take
m\leqslant n
minimal such that
f_{m}\asymp f_{n}
. Then
with
g_{j}:=f_{j}/f_{n}\in K^{\times}
and
g:=g_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+g_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}
we have
g\prec 1
and

g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\preccurlyeq 1
, with
g_{j}\prec 1
iff
j<m
.
Replacing
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
by
g_{m},\dots,g_{n}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n}
by
\phi_{m},\dots,\phi_{n}

we arrange
f_{1}\asymp\cdots\asymp f_{n}\asymp 1
. So

f_{1}=c_{1}+\varepsilon_{1},\,\dots,\,f_{n}=c_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}\quad\text{ with
$c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}$ and $\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}\in\smallo$.}

Then
\varepsilon_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}\prec 1
, hence

c_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+c_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}\ =\ f-\big(\varepsilon_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}\!\big)\ \prec\ 1.

Now Lemma
4.5
yields the desired contradiction.
∎

In the rest of this section,
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
. As noted in Section
1
we can then take
{\Lambda=\Lambda_{H}\imag}
where
\Lambda_{H}
is an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear complement
of
\operatorname{I}(H)
in
H
.
We assume
\Lambda
has this form,
and accordingly identify
{\operatorname{U}}
with
K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]
as explained before Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
be such that
f\prec 1
. Then
f\prec_{\operatorname{g}}1
.

Proof.

We have
f=f_{1}\operatorname{e}(h_{1}\imag)+\cdots+f_{n}\operatorname{e}(h_{n}\imag)
with

f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in K
and distinct
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\in\Lambda_{H}
, so
({\mathbb{R}}h_{1}+\cdots+{\mathbb{R}}h_{n})\cap\operatorname{I}(H)=\{0\}
. For
h\in\Lambda_{H}
we have
\operatorname{e}(h\imag)=\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag}
with
\phi\in H
and
\phi^{\prime}=h
.
Hence
f=f_{1}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{1}\imag}+\cdots+f_{n}\operatorname{e}^{\phi_{n}\imag}
where
\phi_{j}\in H
and
\phi_{j}^{\prime}=h_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,n
.
Now Corollary
4.6
yields
{f\prec_{\operatorname{g}}1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
. Then
f\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
iff
f\prec_{\operatorname{g}}{\mathfrak{m}}
.

An application to slots in
H

Until further notice
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is a slot in
H
of
order
r\geqslant 1
.
We also let
A\in K[\der]
have order
r
, and we let
{\mathfrak{m}}
range over the elements of
H^{\times}
such that

v{\mathfrak{m}}\in v(\widehat{h}-H)
. We begin with an important consequence of the bounds on solutions of linear differential
equations in
Section
2
:

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is
Z
-minimal, deep, and special, and that
{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{P})\asymp{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{v}}(A)
. Let

y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
satisfy
A(y)=0
and
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Then
y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

Proposition
1.9
gives an
{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}
, so it is enough to show
y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}
. Accordingly we assume
0<{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}
below.
As
\widehat{h}
is special over
H
, we have
{2(r+1)v{\mathfrak{m}}}\in v(\widehat{h}-H)
, so
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}^{2(r+1)}
.
Then Corollary
2.7
with
n=2(r+1)
,
\eta=\lvert{\mathfrak{v}}\rvert^{-1}
,
\varepsilon=1/r
gives for
j=0,\dots,r
:

y^{(j)}\ \prec\ {\mathfrak{v}}^{-j}{\mathfrak{m}}^{n-j(1+\varepsilon)}\ \preccurlyeq\ {\mathfrak{m}}^{n-j(2+\varepsilon)}\ \preccurlyeq\ {\mathfrak{m}}^{n-r(2+\varepsilon)}\ =\ {\mathfrak{m}}.\qed

Note that if
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
, then
{\operatorname{U}}=K[\operatorname{e}^{H\imag}]\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
contains every
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
with
A(y)=0
. Corollary
4.8
is typically used in combination with the ultimate condition. Here is a first easy application:

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose
\deg P=1
,
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is ultimate,
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}L_{P}=r
, and
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
satisfies
L_{P}(y)=0
and
y\prec 1
. Then
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

We have
y\in{\operatorname{U}}
, so
y\prec_{\operatorname{g}}1
by Lemma
4.7
. If
y=0
we are done, so assume
{y\neq 0}
.
Then (
1.4
)
gives
0<v_{\operatorname{g}}y\in v_{\operatorname{g}}(\ker^{\neq}_{{\operatorname{U}}}L_{P})={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(L_{P})
, hence
v_{\operatorname{g}}y>v(\widehat{h}-H)
by (
1.5
),
so
y\prec_{\operatorname{g}}{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Now Corollary
4.8
yields the conclusion.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Suppose
\deg P=1
,
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is
Z
-minimal, deep, special, and ultimate, and
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}L_{P}=r
.
Let
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
be such that
P(f)=P(g)=0
and
f,g\prec 1
.
Then
({f-g})^{(j)}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for
j=0,\dots,r
and all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

Use Lemmas
4.9
and
4.10
for
A=L_{P}
and
y=f-g
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection we assume that

(P,1,\widehat{h})
is normal and ultimate,
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
, and
L_{P}=A+B
where

B\prec_{\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})}{\mathfrak{v}}^{r+1}A,\qquad{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{v}}(A)\prec^{\flat}1.

Then Lemma
1.5
gives
{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{P})\sim{\mathfrak{v}}
, and
v_{\operatorname{g}}(\ker^{\neq}_{{\operatorname{U}}}A)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(A)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(L_{P})
by (
1.4
).
This yields a variant of Lemma
4.10
, with a similar proof:

Proposition 4.12
.

If
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
and
A(y)=0
,
y\prec 1
,
then
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

The following result will be used in establishing a crucial non-linear version of Corollary
4.11
, namely Proposition
9.14
.

Corollary 4.13
.

If
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is
Z
-minimal, deep, and special, and

y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
is such that
A(y)=0
and
y\prec 1
,
then
y,y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

Use first Proposition
4.12
and then Lemma
4.9
.
∎

So far we didn’t have to name an immediate asymptotic extension of
H
where
\widehat{h}
is located, but for the “complex” version of the above we need to be more specific:
Let
\widehat{H}
be an immediate asymptotic extension of
H
and

\widehat{K}=\widehat{H}[\imag]\supseteq\widehat{H}
a corresponding immediate
\operatorname{d}
-valued extension of
K
. The results in this subsection then go through if instead of

(P,1,\widehat{h})
being a slot in
H
of order
r\geqslant 1
we assume that
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is a slot in
K
of order
r\geqslant 1
with
\widehat{h}\in\widehat{K}\setminus K
, with
{\mathfrak{m}}
now ranging over the elements of
K^{\times}
such that
v{\mathfrak{m}}\in v(\widehat{h}-K)
.

5.
Inverting Linear Differential Operators over Hardy Fields

Given a Hardy field
H
and
A\in H[\der]
we shall construe
A
as
a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator on various spaces of functions.
We wish to construct right-inverses to such operators.
A key assumption here is that
A
splits over

H[\imag]
. This reduces the
construction of such inverses mainly to the case of order
1
, and this case is handled in the first two subsections using suitable twisted integration operators. In the third subsection we put things together and also show how to “preserve reality”. In the last subsection we introduce damping factors. Throughout we pay attention to the continuity of various operators with respect to various norms, for use in Section
6
.

We let
a
range over
{\mathbb{R}}
and
r
over
{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty,\omega\}
. If
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
r-1
and
r+1
have the usual meaning, while for
r\in\{\infty,\omega\}
we set
r-1=r+1:=r
. (This convention is just to avoid case distinctions.) We have the usual absolute value on

{\mathbb{C}}
given by
|a+b\imag|=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
for
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, so for

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we have
|f|\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
.

Integration and some useful norms

For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we define
\der_{a}^{-1}f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
by

\der_{a}^{-1}f(t)\ :=\ \int_{a}^{t}f(s)\,ds\ :=\ \int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{Re}f(s)\,ds+\imag\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{Im}f(s)\,ds,

so
\der_{a}^{-1}f
is the unique
g\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
such that
g^{\prime}=f
and
g(a)=0
.
The integration operator
\der_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]

is
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear and maps
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
into
\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.
For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we have

\big|\der_{a}^{-1}f(t)\big|\leqslant\big(\der_{a}^{-1}|f|\big)(t)\qquad\text{ for all $t\geqslant a$.}

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
. Call
f

integrable at
\infty
if
\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{a}^{t}f(s)\,ds
exists in
{\mathbb{C}}
. In that case we denote this
limit by
\int_{a}^{\infty}f(s)\,ds
and put

\int_{\infty}^{a}f(s)\,ds\ :=\ -\int_{a}^{\infty}f(s)\,ds,

and define
\der_{\infty}^{-1}f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
by

\der_{\infty}^{-1}f(t)\ :=\ \int_{\infty}^{t}f(s)\,ds\ =\ \int_{\infty}^{a}f(s)\,ds+\int_{a}^{t}f(s)\,ds\ =\ \int_{\infty}^{a}f(s)\,ds+\der_{a}^{-1}f(t),

so
\der_{\infty}^{-1}f
is the unique
g\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
such that
g^{\prime}=f
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}g(t)=0
. Note that

(5.1)

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]:\ \text{$f$ is integrable at $\infty$}\big\}

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and that
\der_{\infty}^{-1}
defines a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
operator from this subspace into
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
which maps
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\cap\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}
into

\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
. If
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and

g\in\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{int}}:=\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{a}
with
\lvert f\rvert\leqslant g
as germs in
\mathcal{C}
, then

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}
; in particular,
if
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and
\lvert f\rvert\in\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{int}}
, then

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}
.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we set

\|f\|_{a}\ :=\ \sup_{t\geqslant a}|f(t)|\ \in\ [0,\infty],

so (with
\operatorname{b}
for “bounded”):

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]:\ \|f\|_{a}<\infty\big\}

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear subspace of

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
, and
f\mapsto\|f\|_{a}
is a norm on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
making it a Banach space over
{\mathbb{C}}
.
It is also convenient to define for
t\geqslant a
the seminorm

\|f\|_{[a,t]}\ :=\ \max_{a\leqslant s\leqslant t}|f(s)|

on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
. More generally, let
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we set

\|f\|_{a;r}\ :=\ \max\big\{\|f\|_{a},\dots,\|f^{(r)}\|_{a}\big\}\ \in\ [0,\infty],

so

\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}:=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]:\ \|f\|_{a;r}<\infty\big\}

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear subspace of

\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, and
f\mapsto\|f\|_{a;r}
makes
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
a normed vector space over
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Note that for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we have
\|fg\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ 2^{r}\|f\|_{a;r}\|g\|_{a;r}
,
so
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is a subalgebra of the
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
.
If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
, then
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
with
\|f^{\prime}\|_{a;r}\leqslant\|f\|_{a;r+1}
.

With
{\boldsymbol{i}}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{r})
ranging over
{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}
, let

P=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}
(all
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
) be a polynomial in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\big[Y,Y^{\prime},\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
.
For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we set

P(f)\ :=\ \sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\qquad\text{where $f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}:=f^{i_{0}}(f^{\prime})^{i_{1}}\cdots(f^{(r)})^{i_{r}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]$.}

We also let

\lVert P\rVert_{a}\ :=\ \max_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\,\lVert P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}\in[0,\infty].

Then
\lVert P\rVert_{a}<\infty
iff
P\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
,
and
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{a}
is a norm on the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
.
In the following assume
\lVert P\rVert_{a}<\infty
.
Then for
j=0,\dots,r
such that
\partial P/\partial Y^{(j)}\neq 0
we have

\lVert\partial P/\partial Y^{(j)}\rVert_{a}\ \leqslant\ (\deg_{Y^{(j)}}P)\cdot\lVert P\rVert_{a}.

Moreover:

Lemma 5.1
.

If
P
is homogeneous of degree
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, then

\lVert P(f)\rVert_{a}\ \leqslant\ {d+r\choose r}\cdot\lVert P\rVert_{a}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d}.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
d\leqslant e
in
{\mathbb{N}}
be such that
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}=0
whenever
\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert<d
or
\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>e
. Then
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have

\lVert P(f)\rVert_{a}\ \leqslant\ D\cdot\lVert P\rVert_{a}\cdot\big(\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d}+\cdots+\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{e}\big)

where
D=D(d,e,r):={e+r+1\choose r+1}-{d+r\choose r+1}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
.

Let
B\colon V\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
be a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map from a normed vector space
V
over
{\mathbb{C}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then we set

\|B\|_{a;r}\ :=\ \sup\big\{\|B(f)\|_{a;r}:\ f\in V,\ \|f\|\leqslant 1\big\}\ \in\ [0,\infty],

the
operator norm of
B
.
Hence with the convention
\infty\cdot b:=b\cdot\infty:=\infty
for
b\in[0,\infty]
we have

\|B(f)\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \|B\|_{a;r}\cdot\|f\|\qquad\text{for $f\in V$.}

Note that
B
is continuous iff
\|B\|_{a;r}<\infty
.
If the map
D\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}^{s}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
(
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
) is also
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear, then

\|D\circ B\|_{a;s}\ \leqslant\ \|D\|_{a;s}\cdot\|B\|_{a;r}.

For
r=0
we drop the subscript:
\|B\|_{a}:=\|B\|_{a;0}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

\der-\phi\ :\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a}[\imag],\quad f\mapsto f^{\prime}-\phi f

maps

\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, and its restriction

\der-\phi\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is continuous with operator norm
\|\der-\phi\|_{a;r-1}\leqslant 1+2^{r-1}\|\phi\|_{a;r-1}
.

Let
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
a_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
a
range over
[a_{0},\infty)
. The

{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map

f\mapsto f|_{[a,+\infty)}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}^{r}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}^{r}[\imag]

satisfies
\|f|_{[a,+\infty)}\|_{a;r}\leqslant\|f\|_{a_{0};r}
for
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}^{r}[\imag]
, so it maps

\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}^{r}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
For
{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}^{0}[\imag]}
also denoting its germ at
+\infty
and its restriction
f|_{[a,+\infty)}
, we have:

\displaystyle f\preccurlyeq 1

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\|f\|_{a}<\infty\ \text{for some $a$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\|f\|_{a}<\infty\ \text{for all $a$,}

\displaystyle f\prec 1

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\|f\|_{a}\to 0\ \text{as $a\to\infty$.}

Twisted integration

For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

g\mapsto fg\ :\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag],

which we also denote by
f
.
We now
fix an element
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
, and set
\Phi:=\der_{a}^{-1}\phi
, so

\Phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
,
\Phi(t)=\int_{a}^{t}\phi(s)\,ds
for
t\geqslant a
, and
\Phi^{\prime}=\phi
. Thus
\operatorname{e}^{\Phi},\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
with
(\operatorname{e}^{\Phi})^{\dagger}=\phi
. Consider the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

B:=\operatorname{e}^{\Phi}\circ\ \der_{a}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag],

so

Bf(t)\ =\ \operatorname{e}^{\Phi(t)}\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\,ds\quad\text{ for $f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]$.}

It is easy to check that
B
is a right inverse to
\der-\phi\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
in the sense that
(\der-\phi)\circ B
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
. Note that for

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
we have
Bf(a)=0
, and thus
(Bf)^{\prime}(a)=f(a)
, using
(Bf)^{\prime}=f+\phi B(f)
.
Set
R:=\operatorname{Re}\Phi
and
S:=\operatorname{Im}\Phi
, so
R,S\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}
,
R^{\prime}=\operatorname{Re}\phi
,
S^{\prime}=\operatorname{Im}\phi
, and
R(a)=S(a)=0
. Note also that if
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, then
\operatorname{e}^{\Phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
, so
B
maps

\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
into
\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.

Suppose
\varepsilon>0
and
\operatorname{Re}\phi(t)\leqslant-\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
. Then
-R
has derivative
-R^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
, so
-R
is strictly increasing with image

[-R(a),\infty)=[0,\infty)
and compositional inverse
(-R)^{\operatorname{inv}}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{0}
. Making
the change of variables
-R(s)=u
for
s\geqslant a
, we obtain for
t\geqslant a
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
, and with
s:=(-R)^{\operatorname{inv}}(u)
,

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\,ds\

\displaystyle=\ \int_{0}^{-R(t)}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\frac{1}{-R^{\prime}(s)}\,du,\ \text{ and thus}

\displaystyle|Bf(t)|\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \operatorname{e}^{R(t)}\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{-R(t)}\operatorname{e}^{u}\,du\cdot\|f\|_{[a,t]}\right)\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{[a,t]}

\displaystyle=\ \big[1-\operatorname{e}^{R(t)}\big]\cdot\|f\|_{[a,t]}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{[a,t]}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \|f\|_{[a,t]}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{[a,t]}\ \leqslant\ \|f\|_{a}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{a}.

Thus
B
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
and
B\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

is continuous with operator norm
\|B\|_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\big\|_{a}
.

Next, suppose
\varepsilon>0
and
\operatorname{Re}\phi(t)\geqslant\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
. Then
R^{\prime}(t)\geqslant\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
, so
R(t)\geqslant\varepsilon\cdot(t-a)
for such
t
. Hence if

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, then
\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi}f
is integrable at
\infty
.
Recall from (
5.1
) that
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}
is the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
consisting of the
g\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
that are integrable at
\infty
.
We have
the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear maps

f\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi}f\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}},\qquad\der_{\infty}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{int}}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag],\ \quad f\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{\Phi}f\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag].

Composition yields
the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
B\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
,

Bf(t)\ :=\ \operatorname{e}^{\Phi(t)}\int_{\infty}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\,ds\qquad(f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}).

It is a right inverse to
\der-\phi
in the sense that

(\der-\phi)\circ B
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Note that
R
is strictly increasing with image

[0,\infty)
and compositional inverse
{R^{\operatorname{inv}}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{0}}
.
Making the change of variables
R(s)=u
for
s\geqslant a
, we obtain for
t\geqslant a
and
{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}}
with
s:=R^{\operatorname{inv}}(u)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\infty}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\,ds\

\displaystyle=\ -\int_{R(t)}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\frac{1}{R^{\prime}(s)}\,du,\ \text{ and thus}

\displaystyle|Bf(t)|\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \operatorname{e}^{R(t)}\cdot\left(\int_{R(t)}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-u}\,du\right)\cdot\|f\|_{t}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \|f\|_{t}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{t}\ \leqslant\ \|f\|_{a}\cdot\left\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\right\|_{a}.

Hence
B
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
, and as a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,
B
is continuous with operator norm

\|B\|_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\big\|_{a}
.
If
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, then
B
maps
{\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]}
into

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.

The case that for some
\varepsilon>0
we have
\operatorname{Re}\phi(t)\leqslant-\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
is called the
attractive case
, and the case that for some
\varepsilon>0
we have
\operatorname{Re}\phi(t)\geqslant\varepsilon
for all
t\geqslant a
is called the
repulsive case
. In both cases the above yields a continuous operator
B\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

with operator norm
\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi}\big\|_{a}

which is right-inverse to the operator
\der-\phi\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
.
We denote this operator
B
by
B_{\phi}
if we need to indicate its dependence on
\phi
. Note also its dependence on
a
. In both the attractive and the repulsive case,
B
maps

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
, and if
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, then

B
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
into

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.

Given a Hardy field
H
and
f\in H[\imag]
with
\operatorname{Re}f\succcurlyeq 1
we can choose
a
and a representative of
f
in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
, to be denoted also by
f
, such that
\operatorname{Re}f(t)\neq 0
for all
t\geqslant a
, and then
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
falls either under the attractive case or under the repulsive case. The original germ
f\in H[\imag]
as well as the function
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
is accordingly said to be attractive, respectively repulsive. (This agrees with the terminology of

1.14
.)

Twists and right-inverses of linear operators over Hardy fields

Let
H
be a Hardy field,
K:=H[\imag]
, and let
A\in K[\der]
be a monic operator of
order
r\geqslant 1
,

A\ =\ \der^{r}+f_{1}\der^{r-1}+\cdots+f_{r},\qquad f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in K.

Take a real number
a_{0}
and functions in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
that represent
the germs
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
and to be denoted also by
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
. Whenever we increase below
the value of
a_{0}
, it is understood that we also update the functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
accordingly, by restriction; the same holds for any function on
[a_{0},\infty)
that gets named. Throughout,
a
ranges over
[a_{0},\infty)
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
denote also the restrictions of these functions to
[a,\infty)
, and likewise for any function on
[a_{0},\infty)
that we name. Thus for any
a
we have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

A_{a}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag],\quad y\mapsto y^{(r)}+f_{1}y^{(r-1)}+\cdots+f_{r}y.

Next, let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
be given. It gives rise to the twist

A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}}\in K[\der]
,

A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}}\ :=\ {\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}A{\mathfrak{m}}\ =\ \der^{r}+g_{1}\der^{r-1}+\cdots+g_{r},\qquad g_{1},\dots,g_{r}\in K.

Now [ADH, (5.1.1), (5.1.2), (5.1.3)] gives universal expressions for
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
in terms of
f_{1},\dots,f_{r},{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}
; for example,
g_{1}=f_{1}+r{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
.
Suppose the germ
{\mathfrak{m}}
is represented by a function
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}[\imag]^{\times}
, also denoted by
{\mathfrak{m}}
. Let
{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}
likewise do double duty as the multiplicative inverse of
{\mathfrak{m}}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
. The expressions above can be used to show that the germs
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
are represented by functions in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
, to be denoted also by
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
, such that for all
a
and all
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we have

{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}A_{a}({\mathfrak{m}}y)\ =\ (A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}})_{a}(y),\text{ where }(A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}})_{a}(y)\ :=\ y^{(r)}+g_{1}y^{(r-1)}+\cdots+g_{r}y.

The operator
A_{a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
is surjective: see
[
33
, (10.6.3)]
or
[
87
, §19, I, II]
. We aim to
construct a right-inverse of
A_{a}
on the subspace
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
.
For this, we assume given a splitting of
A
over
K
,

A\ =\ (\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}),\qquad\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\in K.

Take functions in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
, to be denoted also by
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
, that represent the germs
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
. We increase
a_{0}
to arrange

\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\in\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
. Note that for
j=1,\dots,r

the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map
\der-\phi_{j}\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
restricts to a

{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map
A_{j}\colon\mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{j-1}_{a}[\imag]
, so that we obtain a map

A_{1}\circ\cdots\circ A_{r}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
. It is routine to verify that for all sufficiently large
a
we have

A_{a}\ =\ A_{1}\circ\cdots\circ A_{r}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag].

We increase
a_{0}
so that

A_{a}=A_{1}\circ\cdots\circ A_{r}
for all
a
. Note that
A_{1},\dots,A_{r}
depend on
a
, but we prefer not to indicate this dependence notationally.

Now
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
gives over
K
the splitting

A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}}\ =\ (\der-\phi_{1}+{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}+{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}).

Suppose as before that the germ
{\mathfrak{m}}
is represented by a function

{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}[\imag]^{\times}
. With the usual notational conventions we
have
\phi_{j}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
, giving the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map
\widetilde{A}_{j}:=\der-(\phi_{j}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger})\colon\mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{j-1}_{a}[\imag]

for
j=1,\dots,r
, which for all sufficiently large
a
gives, just as for

A_{a}
, a factorization

(A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}})_{a}\ =\ \widetilde{A}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{A}_{r}.

To construct a right-inverse of
A_{a}
we now assume
\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\succcurlyeq 1
.
Then we increase
a_{0}
once more so that for all
t\geqslant a_{0}
,

\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1}(t),\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}(t)\neq 0.

Recall that for
j=1,\dots,r
we have
the continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

B_{j}\ :=\ B_{\phi_{j}}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

from the previous subsection.
The subsection on twisted integration now yields:

Lemma 5.4
.

The continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

A_{a}^{-1}\ :=\ B_{r}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

is a right-inverse of
A_{a}
: it maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, and
A_{a}\circ A_{a}^{-1}
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. For its operator norm we have
\|A_{a}^{-1}\|_{a}\ \leqslant\ \prod_{j=1}^{r}\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}}\big\|_{a}
.

Suppose
A
is real in the sense that
A\in H[\der]
. Then by increasing
a_{0}
we arrange that
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}
. Next, set

\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}\ :=\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{a}\ =\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}:\,\|f\|_{a}<\infty\big\},

an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{a}
. Then the real part

\operatorname{Re}A_{a}^{-1}\ :\ \mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}},\qquad(\operatorname{Re}A_{a}^{-1})(f)\ :=\ \operatorname{Re}\!\big(A_{a}^{-1}(f)\big)

is
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear and maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}
. Moreover, it is right-inverse to
A_{a}
on
\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}
in the sense that
A_{a}\circ\operatorname{Re}A_{a}^{-1}
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}
, and for
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}
,

\|(\operatorname{Re}A_{a}^{-1})(f)\|_{a}\ \leqslant\ \|A_{a}^{-1}(f)\|_{a}.

Damping factors

Here
H
,
K
,
A
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
a_{0}
are as in Lemma
5.4
.
In particular,
r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
,
\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\succcurlyeq 1
, and
a
ranges over
[a_{0},\infty)
.
For later use we choose damping factors
u

to make the operator
uA_{a}^{-1}
more manageable than
A_{a}^{-1}
.
For
j=0,\dots,r
we set

(5.2)

A_{j}^{\circ}\ :=\ A_{1}\circ\cdots\circ A_{j}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag],

with
A_{0}^{\circ}
the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]

and
A_{r}^{\circ}=A_{a}
, and

(5.3)

B_{j}^{\circ}\ :=\ B_{j}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}},

where
B_{0}^{\circ}
is the identity
on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
B_{r}^{\circ}=A_{a}^{-1}
. Then
B_{j}^{\circ}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
{{\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}}\cap{\mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]}}
and
A_{j}^{\circ}\circ B_{j}^{\circ}
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
by Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\times}
. Then for
i=0,\dots,r
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,

(5.4)

\big[u\cdot A_{a}^{-1}(f)\big]^{(i)}\ =\ \sum_{j=r-i}^{r}u_{i,j}\cdot u\cdot B_{j}^{\circ}(f)\quad\text{ in $\mathcal{C}^{r-i}_{a}[\imag]$}

with coefficient functions
u_{i,j}\in\mathcal{C}^{r-i}_{a}[\imag]
given by
u_{i,r-i}=1
, and for
0\leqslant i<r
,

u_{i+1,j}\ =\ \begin{cases}u_{i,r}^{\prime}+u_{i,r}(u^{\dagger}+\phi_{r})&\text{if $j=r$,}\\
u_{i,j}^{\prime}+u_{i,j}(u^{\dagger}+\phi_{j})+u_{i,j+1}&\text{if $r-i\leqslant j<r$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that for
j=1,\dots,r
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have

B_{j}(f)^{\prime}=f+\phi_{j}B_{j}(f)
.
It is obvious that (
5.4
) holds for
i=0
. Assuming (
5.4
) for a certain
i<r
we get

\big[uA_{a}^{-1}(f)\big]{}^{(i+1)}\ =\ \sum_{j=r-i}^{r}u_{i,j}^{\prime}\cdot uB_{j}^{\circ}(f)+\sum_{j=r-i}^{r}u_{i,j}\cdot\big[uB_{j}^{\circ}(f)\big]^{\prime},

and for
j=r-i,\dots,r
,

\big[uB_{j}^{\circ}(f)\big]^{\prime}\ =\ u^{\prime}B_{j}^{\circ}(f)+u\cdot\big[B_{j}^{\circ}(f)\big]^{\prime}\ =\ u^{\dagger}\cdot uB_{j}^{\circ}(f)+uB_{j-1}^{\circ}(f)+\phi_{j}uB_{j}^{\circ}(f),

which gives the desired result.
∎

Let
{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}
be such that
{\mathfrak{v}}(t)>0
for all
t\geqslant a_{0}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}\in H
,
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
. Then we have the convex subgroup

\Delta\ :=\ \big\{\gamma\in v(H^{\times}):\ \gamma=o(v{\mathfrak{v}})\big\}

of
v(H^{\times})
.
We assume that

\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
in the asymptotic field
K
, where
\phi_{j}
and

{\mathfrak{v}}
also denote their germs.

For real
\nu>0
we have

{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\times}
, so

u\ :=\ {\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}|_{[a,\infty)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\times},\qquad\|u\|_{a}<\infty.

In the next proposition
u
has this meaning, a meaning which accordingly varies with
a
.
Recall that
A_{a}^{-1}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]

with
\|A_{a}^{-1}\|_{a}<\infty
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Assume
H
is real closed and
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\nu>r
. Then:

(i)

the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
uA_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
;

(ii)

uA_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is continuous;

(iii)

there is a real constant
c\geqslant 0
such that
\|uA_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\leqslant c
for all
a
;

(iv)

for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have
uA_{a}^{-1}(f)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}\prec 1
;

(v)

\|uA_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.

Proof.

Note that

{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}1
by [ADH, 9.2.10(iv)]. Denoting the germ of
u
also by
u
we have

u\in H
and
u^{\dagger}=\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}1
, in particular,
u^{\dagger}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1/2}
. Note that the

u_{i,j}
from Lemma
5.5
—that is, their germs—lie in
K
.
Induction on
i
gives
u_{i,j}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{-i}
for
r-i\leqslant j\leqslant r
. Hence
uu_{i,j}\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu-i}\prec_{\Delta}1
for
r-i\leqslant j\leqslant r
. Thus for
i=0,\dots,r
we have a real constant

c_{i,a}\ :=\ \sum_{j=r-i}^{r}\|u\,u_{i,j}\|_{a}\cdot\|B_{j}\|_{a}\cdots\|B_{1}\|_{a}\in[0,\infty)

with
\big\|\big[uA_{a}^{-1}(f)\big]{}^{(i)}\big\|_{a}\leqslant c_{i,a}\|f\|_{a}
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Therefore
uA_{a}^{-1}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, and the operator
uA_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is continuous with

\|uA_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ c_{a}:=\max\{c_{0,a},\dots,c_{r,a}\}.

As to (iii), this is because for all
i
,
j
,
\|u\,u_{ij}\|_{a}
is decreasing as a function
of
a
, and
\|B_{j}\|_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}}\big\|_{a}
for all
j
. For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have
A_{a}^{-1}(f)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, so (iv) holds. As to (v),
u\,u_{i,j}\prec 1
gives
\|uu_{ij}\|_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, for all
i
,
j
. In view of
\|B_{j}\|_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}}\big\|_{a}
for all
j
, this gives

c_{i,a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
for
i=0,\dots,r
, so
c_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.
∎

6.
Solving Split-Normal Equations over Hardy Fields

We construct here
solutions of suitable algebraic differential equations over Hardy fields.
These solutions lie in rings
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
(
r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
) and are obtained as
fixed points of certain contractive maps, as is common in solving differential equations. Here we use that
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is a Banach space with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{a;r}
. It will take some effort to
define the right contractions using the operators from Section
5
.

In this section
H
,
K
,
A
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
a_{0}
are as in Lemma
5.4
.
In particular,
H
is a Hardy field,
K=H[\imag]
, and

A=(\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r})\qquad\text{where $r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}$, $\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\in K$, $\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\succcurlyeq 1$.}

Here
a_{0}
is chosen so that we have representatives for
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
, denoted also by
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
.
We let
a
range over
[a_{0},\infty)
.
In addition we assume that
H
is real closed, and that
we are given a germ
{\mathfrak{v}}\in H^{>}
such that
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
and

\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
for the convex subgroup

\Delta\ :=\ \big\{\gamma\in v(H^{\times}):\ \gamma=o(v{\mathfrak{v}})\big\}

of
v(H^{\times})
. We increase
a_{0}
so
that
{\mathfrak{v}}
is represented by a function in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}
, also denoted by
{\mathfrak{v}}
, with
{\mathfrak{v}}(t)>0
for all
t\geqslant a_{0}
.

Constructing fixed points over
H

Consider a differential equation

(
\ast
)

A(y)\ =\ R(y),\qquad y\prec 1,

where
R\in K\{Y\}
has order
\leqslant r
, degree
\leqslant d\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
and weight
\leqslant w\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant r}
, with

R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
. Now
R=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{j}}}
with
{\boldsymbol{j}}
ranging here and below over the tuples
(j_{0},\dots,j_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}
with
|{\boldsymbol{j}}|\leqslant d
and
\|{\boldsymbol{j}}\|\leqslant w
; likewise for
{\boldsymbol{i}}
.
For each
{\boldsymbol{j}}
we take a function in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]

that represents the germ
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\in K
and let
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}
denote
this function as well as its restriction to any
[a,\infty)
.
Thus
R
is represented on
[a,\infty)
by a polynomial

\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
, to be denoted
also by
R
for simplicity. This yields for each
a
an evaluation map

f\mapsto R(f):=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}f^{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\ :\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag].

As in [ADH, 4.2] we also have for every
{\boldsymbol{i}}
the formal partial derivative

R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}\ :=\ \frac{\partial^{|{\boldsymbol{i}}|}R}{\partial^{i_{0}}Y\cdots\partial^{i_{r}}Y^{(r)}}\ \in\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]

with
R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}Y^{{\boldsymbol{j}}}
, all
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
having their germs in
K
.

A
solution of (

∗

‣
6
) on
[a,\infty)
is a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
such that
A_{a}(f)=R(f)
and
f\prec 1
.
One might try to obtain a solution of (

∗

‣
6
) as a fixed point of the operator
f\mapsto A_{a}^{-1}\big(R(f)\big)
, but this operator might fail to be contractive
on a useful space of functions. Therefore we twist
A
and arrange things so that we can use Proposition
5.6
.
In the rest of this section we fix
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\nu>w
(so
\nu>r
) such that

R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
and
\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\not\sim\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}
in
H

for
j=1,\dots,r
. (Note that such
\nu
exists.) Then the twist

\widetilde{A}:=A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}}={\mathfrak{v}}^{-\nu}A{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}\in K[\der]
splits over
K
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}\

\displaystyle=\ (\der-\phi_{1}+\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}+\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}),\quad\text{ with }

\displaystyle\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}\ {\mathfrak{v}}^{-1},\quad\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\ \succcurlyeq\ 1\qquad(j=1,\dots,r).

We also increase
a_{0}
so that

\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}(t)-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}(t)\neq 0
for all
t\geqslant a_{0}

and such that for all
a
and
u:={\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}|_{[a,\infty)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\times}
the operator
\widetilde{A}_{a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
satisfies

\widetilde{A}_{a}(y)\ =\ u^{-1}A_{a}(uy)\qquad(y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]).

(See the explanations before Lemma
5.4
for definitions of

A_{a}
and
\widetilde{A}_{a}
.) We now increase
a_{0}
once more, fixing it for the rest of the section except in the subsection “Preserving reality”, so as to obtain as in Lemma
5.4
, with
\widetilde{A}
in the role of
A
, a
right-inverse
\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
for such
\widetilde{A}_{a}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have a continuous operator
(
not necessarily
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
)

\Xi_{a}\ :\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}},\quad f\mapsto u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\big(u^{-1}R(f)\big).

It has the property that
\Xi_{a}(f)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}\prec 1

and
A_{a}\big(\Xi_{a}(f)\big)=R(f)

for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.

Proof.

We have
\|u^{-1}R_{\boldsymbol{i}}\|_{a}<\infty
for all
{\boldsymbol{i}}
, so

u^{-1}R(f)=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}u^{-1}R_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, and thus
u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\big(u^{-1}R(f)\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
for such
f
, by Proposition
5.6
(i).
Continuity of
\Xi_{a}
follows from Proposition
5.6
(ii) and continuity of
f\mapsto u^{-1}R(f)\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have
\Xi_{a}(f)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}\prec 1
by Proposition
5.6
(iv), and

u^{-1}A_{a}\big(\Xi_{a}(f)\big)\ =\ u^{-1}A_{a}\big[u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\big(u^{-1}R(f)\big)\big]\ =\ \widetilde{A}_{a}\big[\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\big(u^{-1}R(f)\big)\big]\ =\ u^{-1}R(f),

so
A_{a}\big(\Xi_{a}(f)\big)=R(f)
.
∎

By Lemma
6.1
, each
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
with
\Xi_{a}(f)=f
is a solution of (

∗

‣
6
) on
[a,\infty)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

There is a constant
C_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
such that for all
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,

\|\Xi_{a}(f+g)-\Xi_{a}(f)\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|g\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

We can take these
C_{a}
such that
C_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, and we do so below.

Proof.

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. We have the Taylor expansion

R(f+g)\ =\ \sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{i}}!}R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}(f)g^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\ =\ \sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{i}}!}\bigg[\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}f^{\boldsymbol{j}}\bigg]g^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}.

Now for all
{\boldsymbol{i}}
,
{\boldsymbol{j}}
we have
R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
in
K
, so

u^{-1}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}\prec 1
. Hence

D_{a}\ :=\ \sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|u^{-1}R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|_{a}\ \in\ [0,\infty)

has the property that
D_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, and

\big\|u^{-1}\big(R(f+g)-R(f)\big)\big\|_{a}\ \leqslant\ D_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|g\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

So
h:=u^{-1}\big(R(f+g)-R(f)\big)\in\mathcal{C}^{0}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
gives
\Xi_{a}(f+g)-\Xi_{a}(f)=u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(h)
, and

\|\Xi_{a}(f+g)-\Xi_{a}(f)\|_{a;r}=\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(h)\|_{a;r}\leqslant\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\cdot\|h\|_{a}.

Thus the lemma holds for
C_{a}:=\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\cdot D_{a}
.
∎

In the proof of the next theorem we use the well-known fact that the normed vector space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
is actually a Banach space.
Thus if
S\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is nonempty and closed and
\Phi\colon S\to S
is
contractive (that is, there is a
\lambda\in[0,1)
such that
{\|\Phi(f)-\Phi(g)\|_{a;r}\leqslant\lambda\|f-g\|_{a;r}}

for all
f,g\in S
), then
\Phi
has a unique fixed point
f_{0}
, and
\Phi^{n}(f)\to f_{0}
as
n\to\infty
, for every
f\in S
. (See, for example,
[
87
, Ch. II, §5, IX]
.)

Theorem 6.3
.

For all sufficiently large
a
the operator
\Xi_{a}
maps the closed ball

\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]:\ \|f\|_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

of the Banach space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into itself and has a unique fixed point on this ball.

Proof.

We have
\Xi_{a}(0)=u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(u^{-1}R_{0})
, so
\|\Xi_{a}(0)\|_{a;r}\leqslant\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\|u^{-1}R_{0}\|_{a}
. Now
\|u^{-1}R_{0}\|_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, so by Proposition
5.6
(iii) we can take
a

so large that
\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\|_{a;r}\|u^{-1}R_{0}\|_{a}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
.
For
f
,
g
in the closed ball above we have by Lemma
6.2
,

\|\Xi_{a}(f)-\Xi_{a}(g)\|_{a;r}\ =\ \|\Xi_{a}(f+(g-f))-\Xi_{a}(f)\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot d\|f-g\|_{a;r}.

Take
a
so large that also
C_{a}d\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Then
\|\Xi_{a}(f)-\Xi_{a}(g)\|_{a;r}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|f-g\|_{a;r}
.
Applying this to
g=0
we see that
\Xi_{a}
maps the closed ball above to itself. Thus
\Xi_{a}
has a unique
fixed point on this ball.
∎

If
\deg R\leqslant 0
(so
R=R_{0}
), then
\Xi_{a}(f)=u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(u^{-1}R_{0})
is independent of
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, so for sufficiently large
a
, the fixed point
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
of
\Xi_{a}
with
{\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2}
is
f=\Xi_{a}(0)=u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(u^{-1}R_{0})
.

Next we investigate the difference between solutions of (

∗

‣
6
) on
[a_{0},\infty)
:

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
A_{a_{0}}(f)=R(f)
,
A_{a_{0}}(g)=R(g)
. Then there are positive reals

E
,
\varepsilon
such that for all
a

there exists an
h_{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
with the property that
for
\theta_{a}:=(f-g)|_{[a,\infty)}
,

A_{a}(h_{a})=0,\quad\theta_{a}-h_{a}\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w},\quad\|\theta_{a}-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E\cdot\|{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{a}\cdot\big(\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}^{d}\big),

and thus
h_{a}\prec 1
in case
f-g\prec 1
.

Proof.

Set
\eta_{a}:=A_{a}(\theta_{a})=R(f)-R(g)
, where
f
and
g
stand for their restrictions to
[a,\infty)
. From
R\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
we get

u^{-1}R(f)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
u^{-1}R(g)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, so

u^{-1}\eta_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. By Proposition
5.6
(i),(iv) we have

\xi_{a}\ :=\ u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(u^{-1}\eta_{a})\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}},\qquad\xi_{a}\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}.

Now
\widetilde{A}_{a}(u^{-1}\xi_{a})=u^{-1}\eta_{a}
, that is,

A_{a}(\xi_{a})=\eta_{a}
. Note that then
h_{a}:=\theta_{a}-\xi_{a}
satisfies

A_{a}(h_{a})=0
.
Now
\xi_{a}=\theta_{a}-h_{a}
and
\xi_{a}=\Xi_{a}(g+\theta_{a})-\Xi_{a}(g)
, hence by Lemma
6.2
and its proof,

\displaystyle\|\theta_{a}-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\ =\ \|\xi_{a}\|_{a;r}\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|\theta\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|\theta\|_{a;r}^{d}\big),\text{ with}

\displaystyle C_{a}\

\displaystyle:=\ \big\|u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\big\|_{a;r}\cdot\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|u^{-1}R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|_{a}.

Take a real
\varepsilon>0
such that
R\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu+\varepsilon}
. This gives
a real
e>0
such that
\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}},{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|u^{-1}R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\big\|_{a}\leqslant e\|{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{a}
for all
a
. Now use Proposition
5.6
(iii).
∎

The situation we have in mind in the lemma above is that
f
and
g
are close at infinity, in the sense that
\|f-g\|_{a;r}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.
Then the lemma yields solutions of
A(y)=0
that are
very
close to
f-g
at infinity.
However, being very close at infinity as stated in Lemma
6.4
, namely
\theta_{a}-h_{a}\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
and the rest, is too weak for later use.
We take up this issue again in Section
9
below.
(In Corollary
6.13
later in the present section we already show: if
f\neq g
as germs, then
h_{a}\neq 0
for sufficiently large
a
.)

Preserving reality

We now assume in addition that
A
and
R
are real, that is,
{A\in H[\der]}
and
R\in H\{Y\}
. It is not clear that the fixed points constructed in the proof of Theorem
6.3
are then also real. Therefore we slightly modify this construction using real parts.
We first apply the discussion following Lemma
5.4
to
\widetilde{A}
as well as to
A
, increasing
a_{0}
so that
for all
a
the
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear real part
{\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}
and is right-inverse to
\widetilde{A}_{a}
on
(\mathcal{C}^{0}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
, with

\big\|(\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1})(f)\big\|_{a}\leqslant\big\|\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(f)\big\|_{a}\qquad\text{ for all $f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}^{\operatorname{b}}$.}

Next we set

(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}:\ \|f\|_{a;r}<\infty\big\}\ =\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a},

which is a real Banach space with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{a;r}
.
Finally, this increasing of
a_{0}
is done so that the original
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
restrict to updated functions
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}
.
For all
a
, take
u
,
\Xi_{a}
as in Lemma
6.1
. This lemma has the following real analogue as a consequence:

Lemma 6.5
.

The operator

\operatorname{Re}\,\Xi_{a}\ :\ (\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}},\quad f\mapsto\operatorname{Re}\!\big(\Xi_{a}(f)\big)

satisfies
(\operatorname{Re}\,\Xi_{a})(f)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
for
f\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
, and
any fixed point of
\operatorname{Re}\,\Xi_{a}
is a solution of (

∗

‣
6
) on
[a,\infty)
.

Below the constants
C_{a}
are as in Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For
f,g\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
,

\big\|(\operatorname{Re}\,\Xi_{a})(f+g)-(\operatorname{Re}\Xi_{a})(f)\big\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|g\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

The next corollary is derived from Lemma
6.6
in the same way as Theorem
6.3
from Lemma
6.2
:

Corollary 6.7
.

For all sufficiently large
a
the operator
\operatorname{Re}\,\Xi_{a}
maps the closed ball

\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}:\ \|f\|_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

of the Banach space
(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
into itself and has a unique fixed point on this ball.

We also have a real analogue of Lemma
6.4
:

Corollary 6.8
.

Suppose
f,g\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\operatorname{b}}
and
A_{a_{0}}(f)=R(f)
,
A_{a_{0}}(g)=R(g)
. Then there are positive reals
E
,
\varepsilon
such that
for all
a
there exists an
h_{a}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
with the property that
for
\theta_{a}:=(f-g)|_{[a,\infty)}
,

A_{a}(h_{a})=0,\quad\theta_{a}-h_{a}\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w},\quad\|\theta_{a}-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E\cdot\|{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{a}\cdot\big(\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

Proof.

Take
h_{a}
to be the real part of an
h_{a}
as in Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Some useful bounds

To prepare for Section
9
we derive in this subsection some bounds from Lemmas
6.2
and
6.4
. Throughout we assume
d,r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
.
We begin with an easy inequality:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(V,\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert)
be a normed
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space, and
f,g\in V
. Then

\lVert f+g\rVert^{d}\ \leqslant\ 2^{d}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f\rVert^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert g\rVert^{d}\big\}.

Proof.

Use that

\lVert f+g\rVert\leqslant\lVert f\rVert+\lVert g\rVert\leqslant 2\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f\rVert\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert g\rVert\big\}
.
∎

Now let
u
,
\Xi_{a}
be as in Lemma
6.1
. By that lemma, the operator

\Phi_{a}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\times\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}},\quad(f,y)\mapsto\Xi_{a}(f+y)-\Xi_{a}(f)

is continuous.
Furthermore
\Phi_{a}(f,0)=0
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and

(6.1)

\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\ =\ \Phi_{a}(f+g,y)\qquad\text{for $f,g,y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}$.}

Lemma 6.10
.

There are
C_{a},C_{a}^{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
such that for all
f,g,y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,

(6.2)

\lVert\Phi_{a}(f,y)\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|y\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|y\|_{a;r}^{d}\big),

(6.3)

\lVert\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \\
C_{a}^{+}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|y\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|y\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

We can take these
C_{a},C_{a}^{+}
such that
C_{a},C_{a}^{+}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, and do so below.

Proof.

The
C_{a}
as in Lemma
6.2
