satisfy the requirements on the
C_{a}
here.
Now let
f,g,y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then
by (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) we have

\lVert\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f+g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|y\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|y\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

Thus by Lemma
6.9
,

C_{a}^{+}:=2^{d}\cdot C_{a}
has the required property.
∎

Next, let
f
,
g
be as in the hypothesis of Lemma
6.4
and take
E
,
\varepsilon
, and
h_{a}
(for each
a
) as in its conclusion.
Thus for all
a
and
\theta_{a}:=(f-g)|_{[a,\infty)}
,

\|\theta_{a}-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E\cdot\|{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{a}\cdot\big(\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|\theta_{a}\|_{a;r}^{d}\big),

and if
f-g\prec 1
, then
h_{a}\prec 1
. So

\|\theta_{a}-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E\cdot\|{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\|_{a}\cdot\big(\rho+\cdots+\rho^{d}\big),\quad\rho:=\lVert f-g\rVert_{a_{0};r}.

We let

B_{a}\ :=\ \big\{y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}:\ \lVert y-h_{a}\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

be the closed ball of radius
1/2
around
h_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Using
{\mathfrak{v}}^{\varepsilon}\prec 1
we take
a_{1}\geqslant a_{0}
so that
\theta_{a}\in B_{a}
for all
a\geqslant a_{1}
.
Then for
a\geqslant a_{1}
we have

\lVert h_{a}\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \lVert h_{a}-\theta_{a}\rVert_{a;r}+\lVert\theta_{a}\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2}+\rho,

and hence for
y\in B_{a}
,

(6.4)

\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \lVert y-h_{a}\rVert_{a;r}+\lVert h_{a}\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2}+\big(\frac{1}{2}+\rho\big)\ =\ 1+\rho.

Consider now the continuous operators

\Phi_{a},\Psi_{a}\ :\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}},\qquad\Phi_{a}(y):=\Xi_{a}(g+y)-\Xi_{a}(g),\quad\Psi_{a}(y):=\Phi_{a}(y)+h_{a}.

In the notation introduced above,
\Phi_{a}(y)=\Phi_{a}(g,y)
for
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. With
\xi_{a}
as in the proof of Lemma
6.4

we also have
\Phi_{a}(\theta_{a})=\xi_{a}
and

\Psi_{a}(\theta_{a})=\xi_{a}+h_{a}=\theta_{a}
. Below we reconstruct the fixed point
\theta_{a}
of
\Psi_{a}
from
h_{a}
, for sufficiently large
a
.

Lemma 6.11
.

There exists
a_{2}\geqslant a_{1}
such that for all
a\geqslant a_{2}
we have

\Psi_{a}(B_{a})\subseteq B_{a}
, and
\lVert{\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)}\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|y-z\|_{a;r}
for all
y,z\in B_{a}
.

Proof.

Take
C_{a}
as in Lemma
6.10
, and let
y\in B_{a}
. Then by (
6.2
),

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{a}(y)\|_{a;r}\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ C_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|y\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|y\|_{a;r}^{d}\big),\quad\theta_{a}\in B_{a},\text{ so}

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{a}(y)-h_{a}\|_{a;r}\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ C_{a}M,\quad M:=\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a_{0};r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big((1+\rho)+\cdots+(1+\rho)^{d}\big).

Recall that
C_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
. Suppose
a\geqslant a_{1}
is so large that
C_{a}M\leqslant 1/2
. Then
\Psi_{a}(B_{a})\subseteq B_{a}
.
With
C_{a}^{+}
as in Lemma
6.10
, (
6.3
) gives for
y,z\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,

\lVert\Phi_{a}(y)-\Phi_{a}(z)\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant\\
C^{+}_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|z\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(\|y-z\|_{a;r}+\cdots+\|y-z\|_{a;r}^{d}\big).

Hence with
N:=\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a_{0};r}^{d}\big\}\cdot(1+\rho)^{d}\cdot d
we obtain for
y,z\in B_{a}
that

\lVert\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ C^{+}_{a}N\|y-z\|_{a;r},

so

\lVert{\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)}\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|{y-z}\|_{a;r}
if
C_{a}^{+}N\leqslant 1/2
.
∎

Below
a_{2}
is as in Lemma
6.11
.

Corollary 6.12
.

If
a\geqslant a_{2}
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.

Proof.

Let
a\geqslant a_{2}
. Then
\Psi_{a}
has a unique fixed point on
B_{a}
. As
\Psi_{a}(\theta_{a})=\theta_{a}\in B_{a}
, this fixed point is
\theta_{a}
.
∎

Corollary 6.13
.

If
f\neq g
as germs, then
h_{a}\neq 0
for
a\geqslant a_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
a\geqslant a_{2}
. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
. If
h_{a}=0
, then
\Psi_{a}=\Phi_{a}
, and hence
\theta_{a}=0
, since
\Phi_{a}(0)=0
.
∎

7.
Smoothness Considerations

We assume
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
in this section.
We prove here as much smoothness of solutions of algebraic differential equations over Hardy fields as could be hoped for.
In particular, the solutions in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
of the equation (

∗

‣
6
) in Section
6
actually have their germs in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
.
To make this precise, consider
a “differential” polynomial

P\ =\ P(Y,\dots,Y^{(r)})\ \in\ \mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big].

We put
differential
in quotes since
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]
is not naturally
a differential ring. Nevertheless,
P
defines an obvious evaluation map

f\mapsto P\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)\ :\ \mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}[\imag].

We also have the “separant” of
P
:

S_{P}\ :=\ \frac{\partial P}{\partial Y^{(r)}}\ \in\ \mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big].

Proposition 7.1
.

Assume
n\geqslant 1
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
be such that

P\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)=0\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{P}\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]^{\times}.

Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n+r}[\imag]
.
Thus if
P\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
.
Moreover, if
P\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
, and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
.

We deduce this from the lemma below, which has a complex-analytic hypothesis. Let
{U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{1+r}}
be open. Let
t
range over
{\mathbb{R}}
and

z_{0},\dots,z_{r}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
, and set
x_{j}:=\operatorname{Re}z_{j}
,
y_{j}:=\operatorname{Im}z_{j}
for
j=0,\dots,r
, and

U(t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r-1})\ :=\ \big\{z_{r}:(t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r-1},z_{r})\in U\big\},

an open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
. Assume
\Phi\colon U\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
n\geqslant 1
are such that

\operatorname{Re}\Phi,\operatorname{Im}\Phi\colon U\to{\mathbb{R}}

are
\mathcal{C}^{n}
-functions of

(t,x_{0},y_{0},\dots,x_{r},y_{r})
, and for all
t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r-1}
the function

z_{r}\mapsto\Phi(t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r-1},z_{r})\ \colon\ U(t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r-1})\to{\mathbb{C}}

is holomorphic (the complex-analytic hypothesis alluded to).

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
be a nonempty open interval and suppose
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}(I)[\imag]
is such that for all
t\in I
,

•

\big(t,f(t),\dots,f^{(r)}(t)\big)\in U
;

•

\Phi\big(t,f(t),\dots,f^{(r)}(t)\big)=0
; and

•

(\partial\Phi/\partial z_{r})\big(t,f(t),\dots,f^{(r)}(t)\big)\neq 0
.

Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n+r}(I)[\imag]
.

Proof.

Set
A:=\operatorname{Re}\Phi,\ B:=\operatorname{Im}\Phi
and
g:=\operatorname{Re}f,\ h:=\operatorname{Im}f
. Then for all
t\in I
,

\displaystyle A\big(t,g(t),h(t),g^{\prime}(t),h^{\prime}(t)\dots,g^{(r)}(t),h^{(r)}(t)\big)\

\displaystyle=\ 0

\displaystyle B\big(t,g(t),h(t),g^{\prime}(t),h^{\prime}(t)\dots,g^{(r)}(t),h^{(r)}(t)\big)\

\displaystyle=\ 0.

Consider the
\mathcal{C}^{n}
-map
(A,B)\colon U\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, with
U
identified in the usual way with an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2(1+r)}
. The Cauchy-Riemann equations give

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{r}}\ =\ \frac{\partial A}{\partial x_{r}}+\imag\frac{\partial B}{\partial x_{r}},\qquad\frac{\partial A}{\partial x_{r}}\ =\ \frac{\partial B}{\partial y_{r}},\qquad\frac{\partial B}{\partial x_{r}}\ =\ -\frac{\partial A}{\partial y_{r}}.

Thus the Jacobian matrix of the map
(A,B)
with respect to its last two variables
x_{r}
and
y_{r}
has determinant

D\ =\ \left(\frac{\partial A}{\partial x_{r}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial B}{\partial x_{r}}\right)^{2}\ =\ \left|\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{r}}\right|^{2}\ :\ U\to{\mathbb{R}}.

Let
t_{0}\in I
. Then

D\big(t_{0},g(t_{0}),h(t_{0}),\dots,g^{(r)}(t_{0}),h^{(r)}(t_{0})\big)\neq 0,

so by the Implicit Mapping Theorem
[
33
, (10.2.2), (10.2.3)]
we have a connected open neighborhood
V
of the point

\big(t_{0},g(t_{0}),h(t_{0}),\dots,g^{(r-1)}(t_{0}),h^{(r-1)}(t_{0})\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2r},

open intervals
J,K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
containing
g^{(r)}(t_{0})
,
h^{(r)}(t_{0})
, respectively, and a
\mathcal{C}^{n}
-map

(G,H)\colon V\to J\times K

such that
V\times J\times K\subseteq U
and the zero set of
\Phi
on
V\times J\times K
equals the graph of
(G,H)
.
Take an open subinterval
I_{0}
of
I
with
t_{0}\in I_{0}
such that for all
t\in I_{0}
,

\big(t,g(t),h(t),g^{\prime}(t),h^{\prime}(t),\dots,g^{(r-1)}(t),h^{(r-1)}(t),g^{(r)}(t),h^{(r)}(t)\big)\in V\times J\times K.

Then the above gives that for all
t\in I_{0}
we have

\displaystyle g^{(r)}(t)\ =\ G

\displaystyle\big(t,g(t),h(t),g^{\prime}(t),h^{\prime}(t),\dots,g^{(r-1)}(t),h^{(r-1)}(t)\big),

\displaystyle h^{(r)}(t)\ =\ H

\displaystyle\big(t,g(t),h(t),g^{\prime}(t),h^{\prime}(t),\dots,g^{(r-1)}(t),h^{(r-1)}(t)\big).

It follows easily from these two equalities that
g,h
are of class

\mathcal{C}^{n+r}
on
I_{0}
.
∎

Let
f
continue to be as in Lemma
7.2
. If
\operatorname{Re}\Phi
,
\operatorname{Im}\Phi
are
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then by taking
n
arbitrarily high we conclude that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I)[\imag]
. Moreover:

Corollary 7.3
.

If
\operatorname{Re}\Phi
,
\operatorname{Im}\Phi
are real-analytic, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\omega}(I)[\imag]
.

Proof.

Same as that of Lemma
7.2
, with the reference to
[
33
, (10.2.3)]
replaced by
[
33
, (10.2.4)]
to obtain that
G
,
H
are real-analytic, and noting that then the last displayed relations for
t\in I_{0}
force
g
,
h
to be real-analytic on
I_{0}
by
[
33
, (10.5.3)]
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
I\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
be a nonempty open interval,
n\geqslant 1
,
and

P\ =\ P\big(Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big)\ \in\ \mathcal{C}^{n}(I)[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big].

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}(I)[\imag]
be such that

P\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)=0\in\mathcal{C}(I)[\imag]\quad\text{ and }\quad(\partial P/\partial Y^{(r)})\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)\in\mathcal{C}(I)[\imag]^{\times}.

Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n+r}(I)[\imag]
.
Moreover, if
P\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I)[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I)[\imag]
, and likewise with

\mathcal{C}^{\omega}(I)[\imag]
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I)[\imag]
.

Proof.

Let
P=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}
where all
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(I)[\imag]
. Set

U:=I\times{\mathbb{C}}^{1+r}
, and consider the map
\Phi\colon U\to{\mathbb{C}}
given by

\Phi(t,z_{0},\dots,z_{r}):=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(t)z^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\quad\text{where $z^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}:=z_{0}^{i_{0}}\cdots z_{r}^{i_{r}}$ for ${\boldsymbol{i}}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}$.}

From Lemma
7.2
we obtain
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n+r}(I)[\imag]
. In view of Corollary
7.3
and the remark preceding it, and replacing
n
by
\infty
respectively
\omega
, this argument also gives the second part of the lemma.
∎

Proposition
7.1
follows from Lemma
7.4
by taking suitable representatives of the germs involved.
Let now
H
be a Hardy field and
P\in H[\imag]\{Y\}
of order
r
. Then
P\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
, and so
P(f):=P\big(f,\dots,f^{(r)}\big)\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]

as explained in the beginning of this section.

For notational convenience we set

\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}:=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]:f,f^{\prime},\dots,f^{(n)}\preccurlyeq 1\big\},\qquad(\mathcal{C}^{n})^{\preccurlyeq}:=\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}\cap\mathcal{C}^{n},

and likewise with
\prec
instead of
\preccurlyeq
.
Then
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]
and
(\mathcal{C}^{n})^{\preccurlyeq}
is
an
{\mathbb{R}}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{C}^{n}
. Also,
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\prec}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
,
and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{n}
instead of
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]
.
We have
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}\supseteq\mathcal{C}^{n+1}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
and

(\mathcal{C}^{n})^{\preccurlyeq}\supseteq(\mathcal{C}^{n+1})^{\preccurlyeq}
, and likewise with
\prec
instead of
\preccurlyeq
.
For
R\in\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
we put
R_{\geqslant 1}:=R-R(0)
.

Corollary 7.5
.

Suppose

P=Y^{(r)}+f_{1}Y^{(r-1)}+\cdots+f_{r}Y-R\quad\text{ with $f_{1},\dots,f_{r}$ in~$H[\imag]$ and $R_{\geqslant 1}\prec 1$.}

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
be such that
P(f)=0
. Then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
. Moreover, if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
, and if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
, then
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
.

Proof.

We have
S_{P}=\frac{\partial P}{\partial Y^{(r)}}=1-S
with
S:=\frac{\partial R_{\geqslant 1}}{\partial Y^{(r)}}\prec 1

and thus

S_{P}(f,\dots,f^{(r)})\ =\ 1-S(f,\dots,f^{(r)}),\qquad S(f,\dots,f^{(r)})\prec 1,

so

S_{P}(f,\dots,f^{(r)})\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]^{\times}
. Now appeal to Proposition
7.1
.
∎

Thus the germ of any solution on
[a,\infty)
of the asymptotic equation (

∗

‣
6
) of Section
6
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
, and even in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
(respectively
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
) if
H
is in addition a

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field (respectively, a
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
-Hardy field).

For the differential subfield
K:=H[\imag]
of the differential ring
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have:

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
,
P(f)=0
, and
f
generates a differential subfield
K\langle f\rangle
of
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
over
K
.
If
H
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field, then
{K\langle f\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]}
, and likewise with

\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Suppose
H
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field; it suffices to show
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
.
We may assume that
P
is a minimal annihilator of
f
over
K
; then
S_{P}(f)\neq 0
in
K\langle f\rangle

and so
S_{P}(f)\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]^{\times}
. Hence the claim follows from Proposition
7.1
.
∎

With
H
replacing
K
in this proof we obtain the “real” version:

Corollary 7.7
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
is hardian over
H
and
P(f)=0
for some
P
in
H\{Y\}^{\neq}
.
Then
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\ \Rightarrow\ f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, and
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}\ \Rightarrow\ f\in\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
.

This leads to:

Corollary 7.8
.

Suppose
H
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field. Then every
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic Hardy field extension of
H
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field; in particular,
\operatorname{D}(H)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. Likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
replaced by
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
.

In particular,
\operatorname{D}({\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}

[
22
, Theorems 14.3, 14.9]
.
Let now
H
be a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field. Then by Corollary
7.8
,

H
is
\operatorname{d}
-maximal iff
H
has no proper
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field extension; thus every
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-maximal Hardy field is
\operatorname{d}
-maximal, and
H
has a
\operatorname{d}
-maximal
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field extension. The same remarks apply with
\omega
in place of
\infty
.

8.
Application to Filling Holes in Hardy Fields

This section combines the analytic material above with results from
[
8
]
, some of it summarized in
Lemma
1.10
.
Throughout

H
is a Hardy field with
H\not\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
, and
{r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}}
.
Thus
K:=H[\imag]\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
H
.
(Later we impose extra assumptions on
H
like being real closed with asymptotic integration.)
Note that
{v(H^{\times})\neq\{0\}}
: take
f\in H\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
; then
f^{\prime}\neq 0
, and if
f\asymp 1
, then
f^{\prime}\prec 1
.

Evaluating differential polynomials at germs

Any
Q\in K\{Y\}
of order
\leqslant r
can be evaluated at any
germ
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
to give a germ
Q(y)\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]
,
with
Q(y)\in\mathcal{C}
for
Q\in H\{Y\}
of order
\leqslant r
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}
.
(See the beginning of Section
7
.)
Here is a variant that we shall need. Let
\phi\in H^{\times}
; with
\der
denoting the derivation
of
K
, the derivation
of the differential field
K^{\phi}
is then
\derdelta:=\phi^{-1}\der
. We also let
\derdelta
denote its extension
f\mapsto\phi^{-1}f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}[\imag]
, which maps
\mathcal{C}^{n+1}[\imag]
into
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]
and

\mathcal{C}^{n+1}
into
\mathcal{C}^{n}
, for all
n
. Thus for

j\leqslant r
we have the maps

\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \derdelta\ \ }\ \mathcal{C}^{r-1}[\imag]\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \derdelta\ \ }\ \cdots\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \derdelta\ \ }\ \mathcal{C}^{r-j+1}[\imag]\ \xrightarrow{\ \ \derdelta\ \ }\ \mathcal{C}^{r-j}[\imag],

which by composition yield
\derdelta^{j}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{r-j}[\imag]
, mapping
\mathcal{C}^{r}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r-j}
. This allows us to define for

Q\in K^{\phi}\{Y\}
of order
\leqslant r
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]

the germ
Q(y)\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]
by

Q(y):=q\big(y,\derdelta(y),\dots,\derdelta^{r}(y)\big)\quad\text{ where $Q=q\big(Y,\dots,Y^{(r)})\in K^{\phi}\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]$.}

Note that
H^{\phi}
is a differential subfield of
K^{\phi}
, and if
Q\in H^{\phi}\{Y\}
is of order
\leqslant r
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}
, then
Q(y)\in\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in K^{\times}
. Each of the following conditions implies
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
:

(i)

\phi\preccurlyeq 1
and

\derdelta^{0}(y),\dots,\derdelta^{r}(y)\preccurlyeq 1
;

(ii)

{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq 1
and
y\in{\mathfrak{m}}\,\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
.

Moreover, if
{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq 1
and
(y/{\mathfrak{m}})^{(0)},\dots,(y/{\mathfrak{m}})^{(r)}\prec 1
, then
y^{(0)},\dots,y^{(r)}\prec 1
.

Proof.

For (i), use the smallness of the derivation of
H
and the transformation formulas in [ADH, 5.7] expressing the iterates of
\der
in terms of iterates of
\derdelta
.
For (ii) and the “moreover” part, set
y={\mathfrak{m}}z
with

z=y/{\mathfrak{m}}
and use the Product Rule and the smallness of the derivation of
K
.
∎

Equations over Hardy fields and over their complexifications

Let
{\phi>0}
be active in
H
. We recall here from
[
9
, Section 3]
how the
the asymptotic field
K^{\phi}=H[\imag]^{\phi}
(with derivation
\derdelta=\phi^{-1}\der
)
is isomorphic to the asymptotic field
K^{\circ}:=H^{\circ}[\imag]
for a certain Hardy field
H^{\circ}
: Let

\ell\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
be such that
\ell^{\prime}=\phi
; then
\ell>{\mathbb{R}}
,
\ell\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
, and
\ell^{\operatorname{inv}}\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
for the compositional inverse
\ell^{\operatorname{inv}}
of
\ell
.
The
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra automorphism
f\mapsto f^{\circ}:=f\circ\ell^{\operatorname{inv}}
of
\mathcal{C}[\imag]
(with inverse
g\mapsto g\circ\ell
) maps
\mathcal{C}^{n}[\imag]
and
\mathcal{C}^{n}

onto themselves, and hence restricts to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
and
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
mapping

\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
onto itself.
Moreover,

(
\der,\circ,\derdelta
)

(f^{\circ})^{\prime}\ =\ (\phi^{\circ})^{-1}(f^{\prime})^{\circ}\ =\ \derdelta(f)^{\circ}\qquad\text{for $f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}[\imag]$.}

Thus we have an isomorphism
f\mapsto f^{\circ}:(\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag])^{\phi}\to\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
of differential rings, and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
.
Then

H^{\circ}\ :=\ \{h^{\circ}:h\in H\}\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{C}^{<\infty}

is a Hardy field,
and
f\mapsto f^{\circ}
restricts to an isomorphism
H^{\phi}\to H^{\circ}
of pre-
H
-fields, and to
an isomorphism
K^{\phi}\to K^{\circ}
of asymptotic fields.
We extend the latter to the isomorphism

Q\mapsto Q^{\circ}\ \colon\ K^{\phi}\{Y\}\to K^{\circ}\{Y\}

of differential rings given by
Y^{\circ}=Y
, which restricts to a differential ring isomorphism
H^{\phi}\{Y\}\to H^{\circ}\{Y\}
. Using the identity (

¶
,
∘
,
d

) it is routine to check that
for
Q\in K^{\phi}\{Y\}
of order
\leqslant r
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
, we have

Q(y)^{\circ}\ =\ (Q^{\circ})(y^{\circ})
.
This allows us to translate algebraic differential equations over
K

into algebraic differential equations over
K^{\circ}
: Let
P\in K\{Y\}
have order
\leqslant r
and let
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
.

Lemma 8.2
.

P(y)^{\circ}=P^{\phi}(y)^{\circ}=P^{\phi\circ}(y^{\circ})
where
P^{\phi\circ}:=(P^{\phi})^{\circ}\in K^{\circ}\{Y\}
, hence

P(y)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ P^{\phi\circ}(y^{\circ})=0.

Moreover,
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ y^{\circ}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ}
, for
{\mathfrak{m}}\in K^{\times}
, so asymptotic side conditions are automatically taken care of under this “translation”. Also, if
\phi\preccurlyeq 1
and
y^{\circ}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
, then
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
, by Lemma
8.1
(i) and (

¶
,
∘
,
d

).

In the rest of this section
H\supseteq{\mathbb{R}}
is real closed with asymptotic integration.

Then
H
is an
H
-field, and
K=H[\imag]
is the algebraic closure of
H
, a
\operatorname{d}
-valued field with small derivation extending
H
,
constant field
{\mathbb{C}}
, and value group
\Gamma:=v(K^{\times})=v(H^{\times})
.

Slots in Hardy fields and compositional conjugation

In this subsection we let
{\phi>0}
be active in
H
; as in the previous subsection we take
\ell\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
such that
\ell^{\prime}=\phi
and use the superscript
\circ
accordingly:
f^{\circ}:=f\circ\ell^{\operatorname{inv}}
for
f\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]
.

Let
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
be a slot in
K
of order
r
, so
\widehat{a}\notin K
is an element of an immediate asymptotic extension
\widehat{K}
of
K
with
P\in Z(K,\widehat{a})
and
\widehat{a}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
. We associate to
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
a slot in
K^{\circ}
as follows:
choose an immediate asymptotic extension
\widehat{K}^{\circ}
of
K^{\circ}
and an
isomorphism
\widehat{f}\mapsto\widehat{f}^{\circ}\colon\widehat{K}^{\phi}\to\widehat{K}^{\circ}
of asymptotic fields
extending the isomorphism
f\mapsto f^{\circ}\colon K^{\phi}\to K^{\circ}
.
Then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is a slot in
K^{\circ}
of the same complexity as
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
. The
equivalence class of
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
does not depend on the choice of

\widehat{K}^{\circ}
and the isomorphism
\widehat{K}^{\phi}\to\widehat{K}^{\circ}
.
If
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is a hole in
K
, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is a hole in
K^{\circ}
, and likewise with “minimal hole” in place of “hole”. By
[
8
, Lemmas 3.1.20, 3.3.20, 3.3.40]
we have:

Lemma 8.3
.

If
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is
Z
-minimal, then so is
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
, and likewise with “quasilinear” and “special” in place of “
Z
-minimal”.
If
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is steep and
{\phi\preccurlyeq 1}
, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is steep,
and likewise with “deep”, “normal”, and “strictly normal” in place of “steep”.

Next, let
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
be a slot in
H
of order
r
, so
\widehat{a}\notin H
is an element of an immediate asymptotic extension
\widehat{H}
of
H
with
P\in Z(H,\widehat{a})
and
\widehat{a}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
.
We associate to
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
a slot in
H^{\circ}
as follows:
choose an immediate asymptotic extension
\widehat{H}^{\circ}
of
H^{\circ}
and an
isomorphism
\widehat{f}\mapsto\widehat{f}^{\circ}\colon\widehat{H}^{\phi}\to\widehat{H}^{\circ}
of asymptotic fields
extending the isomorphism
f\mapsto f^{\circ}\colon H^{\phi}\to H^{\circ}
.
Then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is a slot in
H^{\circ}
of the same complexity as
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
. The
equivalence class of
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
does not depend on the choice of

\widehat{H}^{\circ}
and the isomorphism
\widehat{H}^{\phi}\to\widehat{H}^{\circ}
.
If
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is a hole in
H
, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is a hole in
H^{\circ}
, and likewise with “minimal hole” in place of “hole”.
Lemma
8.3
goes through in this setting. Also, recalling
[
9
, Lemma 4.5]
, if
H
is Liouville closed and
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})

is ultimate, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is ultimate.

Moreover, by
[
8
, Lemmas 4.3.5, 4.3.28 , Corollaries 4.5.23, 4.5.39]
:

Lemma 8.4
.

(i)

If
\phi\preccurlyeq 1
and
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is split-normal, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is split-normal;
likewise with “split-normal” replaced by “strongly split-normal”.

(ii)

If
\phi\prec 1
and
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is
Z
-minimal, deep, and repulsive-normal, then
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{m}}^{\circ},\widehat{a}^{\circ})
is repulsive-normal; likewise with “repulsive-normal” replaced by
“strongly repulsive-normal”.

Reformulations

We reformulate here some results of Sections
6
and
7
to facilitate their use.
For
{\mathfrak{v}}\in K^{\times}
with
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
we set:

\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})\ :=\ \big\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\gamma=o(v{\mathfrak{v}})\big\},

a proper convex subgroup of
\Gamma
.
In the next lemma,
P\in K\{Y\}
has order
r
and
P=Q-R
, where
Q,R\in K\{Y\}
and
Q
is homogeneous of degree
1
and order
r
.
We set
w:=\operatorname{wt}(P)
, so
w\geqslant r\geqslant 1
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Suppose that
L_{Q}
splits strongly over
K
,
{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\prec^{\flat}1
, and

R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})^{w+1}Q,\quad\Delta:=\Delta\big({\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\big).

Then
P(y)=0
and
y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\preccurlyeq 1

for some
y\prec{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})^{w}
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
. Moreover:

(i)

if
P,Q\in H\{Y\}
, then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
;

(ii)

if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then for any
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
with
P(y)=0
we have
y\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
; likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Set
{\mathfrak{v}}:=\lvert{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\rvert\in H^{>}
, so
{\mathfrak{v}}\asymp{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})
. Take
f\in K^{\times}
such that
A:=f^{-1}L_{Q}
is monic; then
{\mathfrak{v}}(A)={\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\asymp{\mathfrak{v}}
and

f^{-1}R\prec_{\Delta}f^{-1}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}Q\asymp{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
.
We have
A=(\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r})
where
\phi_{j}\in K
and

\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}\succcurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\succcurlyeq 1
for
j=1,\dots,r
by the strong splitting assumption. Also
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
by (
1.3
).
The claims now follow from various results in Section
6
applied to the equation
{A(y)=f^{-1}R(y)}
,
y\prec 1
in the role of (

∗

‣
6
), using also Corollary
7.5
. ∎

In the next two lemmas
\phi
is active in
H
with
{0<\phi\preccurlyeq 1}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
(P,{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{h})
be a slot in
H
of order
r
such that

(P^{\phi},{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{h})
is strongly split-normal. Then for some
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
,

P(y)\ =\ 0,\quad y\ \prec\ {\mathfrak{n}},\quad y\in{\mathfrak{n}}\,(\mathcal{C}^{r})^{\preccurlyeq}.

If
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then there exists such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

First we consider the case
\phi=1
.
Replace
(P,{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{h})
by
(P_{\times{\mathfrak{n}}},1,\widehat{h}/{\mathfrak{n}})

to arrange
{\mathfrak{n}}=1
.
Then
L_{P}
has order
r
,
{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{v}}(L_{P})\prec^{\flat}1
, and
P=Q-R
where
Q,R\in H\{Y\}
,

Q
is homogeneous of degree
1
and order
r
,
L_{Q}\in H[\der]
splits strongly over
K
,
and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}P_{1}
, where
\Delta:=\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})
and
w:=\operatorname{wt}(P)
.
Now
P_{1}=Q-R_{1}
, so
{\mathfrak{v}}\sim{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})
by Lemma
1.5
(ii), and thus
\Delta=\Delta\big({\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\big)
.
Lemma
8.5
gives
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
such that
y\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}\prec 1
,

P(y)=0
, and

y^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Then
y
has for
{\mathfrak{n}}=1
the properties displayed in the lemma.

Now suppose
\phi
is arbitrary. Employing
(\phantom{-})^{\circ}
as explained earlier in this section, the
slot
(P^{\phi\circ},{\mathfrak{n}}^{\circ},\widehat{h}^{\circ})
in the Hardy field
H^{\circ}
is strongly split-normal, hence by the case
\phi=1
we have
z\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
with

P^{\phi\circ}(z)=0
,
z\prec{\mathfrak{n}}^{\circ}
, and
(z/{\mathfrak{n}}^{\circ})^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1

for
{j=1,\dots,r}
.
Take
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
with
y^{\circ}=z
. Then

P(y)=0
,
y\prec{\mathfrak{n}}
, and
y\in{\mathfrak{n}}\,(\mathcal{C}^{r})^{\preccurlyeq}
by Lemma
8.2
and a subsequent remark.
Moreover, if
\phi,z\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then
y\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.
∎

In the next “complex” version,
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is a slot in
K
of order
r
with
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Suppose
the slot
(P^{\phi},{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
in
K^{\phi}
is strictly normal, and its linear part splits strongly
over
K^{\phi}
. Then for some
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have

P(y)\ =\ 0,\quad y\ \prec\ {\mathfrak{m}},\quad y\in{\mathfrak{m}}\,\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}.

If
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
.
If
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
, then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
.

Proof.

Consider first the case
\phi=1
. Replacing
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
by
(P_{\times{\mathfrak{m}}},1,\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{m}})

we arrange
{\mathfrak{m}}=1
.
Set
L:=L_{P}\in K[\der]
,
Q:=P_{1}
, and
R:=P-Q
.
Since
(P,1,\widehat{a})
is strictly normal, we have
\operatorname{order}(L)=r
,
{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{v}}(L)\prec^{\flat}1
,
and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}Q
where
\Delta:=\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})
,
w:=\operatorname{wt}(P)
. As
L
splits strongly over
K
, Lemma
8.5
gives
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
such that

P(y)=0
,
y\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}\prec 1
, and
y^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1

for
j=1,\dots,r
. For the last part of the lemma, use the last part of Lemma
8.5
.
The general case reduces to this special case as in the proof of Lemma
8.6
.
∎

Finding germs in holes

In this subsection
\widehat{H}
is an immediate asymptotic extension of
H
.

This fits into the setting of
[
8
, Section 4.3]
on split-normal slots:

K=H[\imag]
and
\widehat{H}
have
H
as a common asymptotic subfield and
\widehat{K}:=\widehat{H}[\imag]
as a common asymptotic extension,
\widehat{H}
is an
H
-field, and
\widehat{K}
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued.

Assume also that
H
is
\upomega
-free.
Then
K
is
\upomega
-free by [ADH, 11.7.23].

Let
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
with
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
and
\widehat{a}\in\widehat{K}\setminus K
be a minimal hole in
K
of order
{r\geqslant 1}
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Suppose
\deg P>1
.
Then for some
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have

P(y)\ =\ 0,\quad y\ \prec\ {\mathfrak{m}},\quad y\in{\mathfrak{m}}\,\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}.

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq 1
, then
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
for such
y
.
Moreover, if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then we can take such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
, and
if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
, then we can take such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
.

Proof.

Lemma
1.10
gives
a refinement
(P_{+a},{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{a}-a)
of
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
with
{\mathfrak{n}}\in H^{\times}
and an active
\phi
in
H
with
0<\phi\preccurlyeq 1
such that
the hole
(P^{\phi}_{+a},{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{a}-a)
in
K^{\phi}
is strictly normal and its linear part splits
strongly over
K^{\phi}
.
Lemma
8.7
applied to
(P_{+a},{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{a}-a)
in place of
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})

yields
z\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
with
P_{+a}(z)=0
,
z\prec{\mathfrak{n}}
and
(z/{\mathfrak{n}})^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Lemma
8.1
(ii) applied to
z/{\mathfrak{m}}
,
{\mathfrak{n}}/{\mathfrak{m}}
in place of
y
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
, respectively, yields
(z/{\mathfrak{m}})^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1
for
j=0,\dots,r
. Also,
a\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
(in
K
), hence
(a/{\mathfrak{m}})^{(j)}\prec 1
for
j=0,\dots,r
. Set
y:=a+z
; then
P(y)=0
,
y\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
, and
(y/{\mathfrak{m}})^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
For the rest use Lemma
8.1
(ii) and the last part of Lemma
8.7
.
∎

Next we treat the linear case:

Proposition 8.9
.

Suppose
\deg P=1
. Then for some
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have

P(y)\ =\ 0,\quad y\ \prec\ {\mathfrak{m}},\quad(y/{\mathfrak{m}})^{\prime}\ \preccurlyeq\ 1.

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\preccurlyeq 1
, then
y\prec 1
and
y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq 1
for each such
y
.
Moreover, if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then we can take such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
, and
if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
, then we can take such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
.

Proof.

We have
r=1
by
[
8
, Corollary 3.2.8]
.
If
\der K=K
and
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, then Lemma
1.11
applies, and we can argue as in the proof of Proposition
8.8
,
using this lemma instead of Lemma
1.10
.
We reduce the general case to this special case as follows:
Set
H_{1}:=\operatorname{D}(H)
; then
H_{1}
is an
\upomega
-free Hardy field by
[
8
, Theorem 1.3.1]
, and
K_{1}:=H_{1}[\imag]
satisfies
\der K_{1}=K_{1}
and
\operatorname{I}(K_{1})\subseteq K_{1}^{\dagger}
by Propositions
2.1
and
2.2
. Moreover, by Corollary
7.8
, if
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then
H_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, and likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place
of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.
The newtonization
\widehat{H}_{1}
of
H_{1}
is an immediate asymptotic extension of
H_{1}
, and
\widehat{K}_{1}:=\widehat{H}_{1}[\imag]
is newtonian [ADH, 14.5.7]. Lemma
1.6
gives an embedding
K\langle\widehat{a}\rangle\to\widehat{K}_{1}
over
K
; let
\widehat{a}_{1}
be the image of
\widehat{a}
under this embedding.
If
\widehat{a}_{1}\in K_{1}
, then we are done by taking
y:=\widehat{a}_{1}
, so we may assume
\widehat{a}_{1}\notin K_{1}
. Then
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a}_{1})
is a minimal hole in
K_{1}
, and the above applies with
H
,
K
,
\widehat{a}
replaced by
H_{1}
,
K_{1}
,
\widehat{a}_{1}
, respectively.
∎

We can improve on these results in a useful way:

Corollary 8.10
.

Suppose

\widehat{a}\sim a\in K
. Then for some
y\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have

P(y)\ =\ 0,\quad y\ \sim\ a,\quad(y/a)^{(j)}\ \prec\ 1\ \text{ for $j=1,\dots,r$.}

If
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
.
If
H\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
, then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}[\imag]
.

Proof.

Take
a_{1}\in K
and
{\mathfrak{n}}\in H^{\times}
such that

{\mathfrak{n}}\asymp\widehat{a}-a\sim a_{1}
, and set
{b:=a+a_{1}}
.
Then
(P_{+b},{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{a}-b)
is a refinement of
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
, and
Propositions
8.8
and
8.9
give
{z\in\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]}

with
P(b+z)=0
,
z\prec{\mathfrak{n}}
and
(z/{\mathfrak{n}})^{(j)}\preccurlyeq 1
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
We have
(a_{1}/a)^{(j)}\prec 1
for
j=0,\dots,r
, since
K
has small derivation.
Likewise,
({\mathfrak{n}}/a)^{(j)}\prec 1
for
j=0,\dots,r
, and hence

(z/a)^{(j)}\prec 1
for
j=0,\dots,r
, by
z/a=(z/{\mathfrak{n}})\cdot({\mathfrak{n}}/a)
and the Product Rule.
So
y:=b+z
has the desired property. The rest follows from the “moreover” parts of these propositions.
∎

Remark 8.11
.

If we replace our standing assumption that
H
is
\upomega
-free and
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is a minimal hole in
K
by the assumption that
H
is
\uplambda
-free,
\der K=K
,
{\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}}
, and
(P,{\mathfrak{m}},\widehat{a})
is a slot in
K
of order and degree
1
,
then Proposition
8.9
and Corollary
8.10
go through by Remark
1.12
.

Can we choose
y
in Corollary
8.10
such that additionally
\operatorname{Re}y
,
\operatorname{Im}y
are hardian over
H
? At this stage we cannot claim this.
In the next section we introduce weights and their corresponding norms as a more refined tool. This will allow us to
obtain Corollary
9.20
as a key approximation result for later use.

9.
Weights

In this section we prove Proposition
9.14
to strengthen
Lemma
6.4
.
This uses the material on repulsive-normal slots from
[
8
, Section 4.5]
, but
we also need more refined norms for differentiable functions, to which we turn now.

The final result, Corollary
9.20
, is the only part of this section used towards our main result, Theorem
11.19
. But this use, in the proof of Theorem
11.10
, is essential, and obtaining this corollary requires the entire section.

Weighted spaces of differentiable functions

In this subsection we fix
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a weight
function
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\times}
.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we set

\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ :=\ \max\big\{\lVert\uptau^{-1}f\rVert_{a},\lVert\uptau^{-1}f^{\prime}\rVert_{a},\dots,\lVert\uptau^{-1}f^{(r)}\rVert_{a}\big\}\ \in\ [0,+\infty],

and
\lVert f\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}:=\lVert f\rVert_{a;0}^{\uptau}
for
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
.
Then

\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]:\,\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}<+\infty\big\}

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear subspace of

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\ :=\ \mathcal{C}^{0}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\ =\ \uptau\,\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\ =\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]:\,f\preccurlyeq\uptau\big\}.

Below we consider the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
to be equipped with the norm

f\mapsto\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

Recall from Section
5
the convention
b\cdot\infty=\infty\cdot b=\infty
for
b\in[0,\infty]
. Note that

(9.1)

\lVert fg\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ 2^{r}\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}\,\lVert g\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\quad\text{ for $f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]$,}

so
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
-submodule of
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
.
Note also that
\lVert 1\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}=\lVert\uptau^{-1}\rVert_{a}
, hence

\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ 2^{r}\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}\,\lVert\uptau^{-1}\rVert_{a}\quad\text{ for $f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]$}

and

\uptau^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 1\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}.

We have

(9.2)

\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ \lVert f\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\,\lVert\uptau\rVert_{a}\quad\text{ for $f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]$,}

and thus

(9.3)

\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau^{-1}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}.

Hence if
\uptau,\uptau^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, then
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}=\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, and the norms

\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}
and
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{a;r}
on this
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space are equivalent.
(In later use,
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,
\uptau^{-1}\notin\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.)
If
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, then
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
is an ideal of the commutative ring
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. From (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) we obtain

\lVert fg\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ 2^{r}\,\lVert\uptau\rVert_{a}\,\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\,\lVert g\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\quad\text{ for $f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]$.}

For
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
we have
\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau},\lVert f^{\prime}\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{a;r+1}^{\uptau}
.
From (
9.2
) and (
9.3
):

Lemma 9.1
.

Suppose
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

(
so
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}})
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. If
(f_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
and

f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, then also
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.

This is used to show:

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then
the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
equipped with the norm

\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}
is complete.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
r
.
Let
(f_{n})
be a cauchy sequence in the normed space
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. Then
the sequence
(\uptau^{-1}f_{n})
in the Banach space

\mathcal{C}^{0}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is cauchy, hence has a limit
g\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, so
with
f:=\uptau g\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
we have

\uptau^{-1}f_{n}\to\uptau^{-1}f
in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and hence
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.
Thus the lemma holds for
r=0
. Suppose the lemma holds for a certain value of

r
, and let
(f_{n})
be a cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.
Then
(f_{n}^{\prime})
is a cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
and hence has a limit

g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, by inductive hypothesis.
By Lemma
9.1
,
f_{n}^{\prime}\to g
in

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Now
(f_{n})
is also a cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, hence has a limit

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
(by the case
r=0
), and
by Lemma
9.1
again,
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Thus

f
is differentiable and
f^{\prime}=g
by
[
33
, (8.6.4)]
.
This yields
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Suppose
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
and
f^{(k)}\preccurlyeq\uptau^{r-k+1}
for
k=0,\dots,r
, then
f\uptau^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
(
Thus
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau^{r+1}}\subseteq\uptau\,\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
)

Proof.

An easy induction on
n\leqslant r
shows that there are
Q^{n}_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n-k}]
(
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
)
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
and
n\leqslant r
:

(f\uptau^{-1})^{(n)}\ =\ \sum_{k=0}^{n}Q^{n}_{k}(\uptau,\uptau^{\prime},\dots,\uptau^{(n-k)})f^{(k)}\uptau^{k-n-1}.

Now use that
Q^{n}_{k}(\uptau,\uptau^{\prime},\dots,\uptau^{(n-k)})\preccurlyeq 1
for
n\leqslant r
and
k=0,\dots,n
.
∎

Next we generalize the inequality (
9.1
):

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{m-1},g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
,
m\geqslant 1
; then

\lVert f_{1}\cdots f_{m-1}g\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ m^{r}\,\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{a;r}\cdots\lVert f_{m-1}\rVert_{a;r}\,\lVert g\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

Proof.

Use the generalized Product Rule [ADH, p. 199] and the well-known identity

\sum\frac{n!}{i_{1}!\cdots i_{m}!}=m^{n}
with the sum over all
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
with
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}=n
.
∎

With
{\boldsymbol{i}}
ranging over
{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}
, let

P=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}Y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}
(all
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
) be a polynomial in
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\big[Y,Y^{\prime},\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
;
for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
we have
P(f)=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
.
(See also the beginning of Section
6
.)
We set

\lVert P\rVert_{a}\ :=\ \max_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\,\lVert P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}\in[0,\infty].

In the rest of this subsection we assume
\lVert P\rVert_{a}<\infty
, that is,
P\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\big[Y,\dots,Y^{(r)}\big]
. Hence if
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
,
P(0)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, then
P(f)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. Here are weighted versions of Lemma
5.1

and
5.2
:

Lemma 9.5
.

Suppose
P
is homogeneous of degree
d\geqslant 1
, and let
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. Then

\lVert P(f)\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ {d+r\choose r}\cdot\lVert P\rVert_{a}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

Proof.

For
j=0,\dots,r
we have
\lVert f^{(j)}\rVert_{a}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}
and
\lVert f^{(j)}\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}
.
Now
f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{i}}=(i_{0},\dots,i_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r+1}
and
i_{0}+\cdots+i_{r}=d
, is a product of
d
such factors
f^{(j)}
, so
Lemma
9.4
with
m:=d
,
r:=0
, gives

\lVert f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}^{\tau}\ \leqslant\ \lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

It remains to note that by (
9.1
) we have
\lVert P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}^{\tau}\leqslant\lVert P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}\cdot\lVert f^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\rVert_{a}^{\tau}
.
∎

Corollary 9.6
.

Let
1\leqslant d\leqslant e
in
{\mathbb{N}}
be such that
P_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}=0
if
\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert<d
or
\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>e
.
Then for
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
and
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have

\lVert P(f)\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ D\cdot\lVert P\rVert_{a}\cdot\big(\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}+\cdots+\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{e-1}\big)\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}

where

D\ =\ D(d,e,r)\ :=\ {e+r+1\choose r+1}-{d+r\choose r+1}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
.

Doubly-twisted integration

In this subsection we adopt the setting in
Twisted integration
of Section
5
.
Thus
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and
\Phi=\der_{a}^{-1}\phi
.
Let
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}
satisfy
{\uptau(s)>0}
for
s\geqslant a
, and set
\widetilde{\phi}:=\phi-\uptau^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and
\widetilde{\Phi}:=\der_{a}^{-1}\widetilde{\phi}
. Thus

\widetilde{\Phi}(t)\ =\ \int_{a}^{t}(\phi-\uptau^{\dagger})(s)\,ds\ =\ \Phi(t)-\log\uptau(t)+\log\uptau(a)\qquad\text{for $t\geqslant a$.}

Consider the right inverses

B,\widetilde{B}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]

to, respectively,
\der-\phi\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and
\der-\widetilde{\phi}\colon\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
,
given by

B\ :=\ \operatorname{e}^{\Phi}\circ\,{\der_{a}^{-1}}\circ\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi},\quad\widetilde{B}\ :=\ \operatorname{e}^{\widetilde{\Phi}}\circ\,{\der_{a}^{-1}}\circ\operatorname{e}^{-\widetilde{\Phi}}.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
and
t\geqslant a
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}f(t)\

\displaystyle=\ \operatorname{e}^{\widetilde{\Phi}(t)}\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\widetilde{\Phi}(s)}f(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\ \uptau(t)^{-1}\uptau(a)\operatorname{e}^{\Phi(t)}\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}\uptau(s)\uptau(a)^{-1}f(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\ \uptau(t)^{-1}\operatorname{e}^{\Phi(t)}\int_{a}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}\uptau(s)f(s)\,ds\ =\ \uptau^{-1}(t)\big(B(\uptau f)\big)(t)

and so
\widetilde{B}=\uptau^{-1}\circ B\circ\uptau
.
Hence if
\widetilde{\phi}
is attractive, then
B_{\ltimes\uptau}:=\uptau^{-1}\circ B\circ\uptau
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
, and
the operator
B_{\ltimes\uptau}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is continuous with
\lVert B_{\ltimes\uptau}\rVert_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{\phi}}\big\|_{a}
; if in addition
\widetilde{\phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, then
B_{\ltimes\uptau}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
into

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.
Note that if
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
and
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}
, then
\widetilde{\phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
.

Next, suppose
\phi
,
\widetilde{\phi}
are both repulsive. Then we have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operators
B,\widetilde{B}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
given, for
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
t\geqslant a
, by

Bf(t)\ :=\ \operatorname{e}^{\Phi(t)}\int_{\infty}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\Phi(s)}f(s)\,ds,\qquad\widetilde{B}f(t)\ :=\ \operatorname{e}^{\widetilde{\Phi}(t)}\int_{\infty}^{t}\operatorname{e}^{-\widetilde{\Phi}(s)}f(s)\,ds.

Now assume
\uptau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then
we have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator

B_{\ltimes\uptau}\ :=\ {\uptau^{-1}\circ B\circ\uptau}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag].

A computation as above shows
\widetilde{B}=B_{\ltimes\uptau}
; thus
B_{\ltimes\uptau}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{a}[\imag]
, and the operator
B_{\ltimes\uptau}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
is continuous with
\lVert B_{\ltimes\uptau}\rVert_{a}\leqslant\big\|\frac{1}{\operatorname{Re}\widetilde{\phi}}\big\|_{a}
.
If
\widetilde{\phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
, then
B_{\ltimes\uptau}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
into

\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r+1}_{a}[\imag]
.

More on twists and right-inverses of linear operators over Hardy fields

In this subsection we adopt the assumptions in force for Lemma
5.4
, which we repeat here.
Thus
H
is a Hardy field,
K=H[\imag]
,
r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
, and
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in K
.
We fix
a_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and functions in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
representing the germs
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
denoted by the same symbols. We let
a
range over
[a_{0},\infty)
, and we denote the restriction of each

f\in\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
to
[a,\infty)
also by
f
.
For each
a
we then have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map
A_{a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
given by

A_{a}(y)\ =\ y^{(r)}+f_{1}y^{(r-1)}+\cdots+f_{r}y.

We are in addition given a splitting
(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r})
of
the linear differential operator
A=\der^{r}+f_{1}\der^{r-1}+\cdots+f_{r}\in K[\der]
over
K

with
\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\succcurlyeq 1
,
as well as functions in
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}^{r-1}[\imag]
representing
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
denoted by the same symbols and satisfying

\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\in(\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}})^{\times}
.
This gives rise to the continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operators

B_{j}\ :=\ B_{\phi_{j}}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\qquad(j=1,\dots,r)

and the right-inverse

A_{a}^{-1}\ :=\ B_{r}\circ\cdots\circ B_{1}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

of
A_{a}
with the properties stated in Lemma
5.4
.

Now let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
with
{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
, and set
\widetilde{A}:=A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{m}}}\in K[\der]
. Let
\uptau\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\times}
be
a representative of
{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then
\uptau\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\operatorname{b}}

and
\widetilde{\phi}_{j}:=\phi_{j}-\uptau^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{C}^{r-1}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
for
j=1,\dots,r
.
We have the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear maps

\widetilde{A}_{j}\ :=\ \der-\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}^{j-1}_{a}[\imag]\qquad(j=1,\dots,r)

and for sufficiently large
a
a factorization

\widetilde{A}_{a}\ =\ \widetilde{A}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{A}_{r}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag].

Below we assume this holds for all
a
, as can be arranged by increasing
a_{0}
.
We call
f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]

alike
if
f
,
g
are both attractive or
both repulsive. In the same way we define when germs
f,g\in\mathcal{C}[\imag]

are alike.
Suppose that
\phi_{j}
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{j}
are alike for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Then we have continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operators

\widetilde{B}_{j}\ :=\ B_{\widetilde{\phi}_{j}}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\qquad(j=1,\dots,r)

and the right-inverse

\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\ :=\ \widetilde{B}_{r}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{B}_{1}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

of
\widetilde{A}_{a}
, and the arguments in the previous subsection show that
\widetilde{B}_{j}=(B_{j})_{\ltimes\uptau}=\uptau^{-1}\circ B_{j}\circ\uptau
for
j=1,\dots,r
, and hence

\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}=\uptau^{-1}\circ A_{a}^{-1}\circ\uptau
.
For
j=0,\dots,r
we set, in analogy with
A_{j}^{\circ}
and
B_{j}^{\circ}
from (
5.2
) and (
5.3
),

\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\circ}\ :=\ \widetilde{A}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{A}_{j}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag],\quad\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\circ}\ :=\ \widetilde{B}_{j}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{B}_{1}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}.

Then
\widetilde{B}_{j}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\cap\mathcal{C}^{j}_{a}[\imag]
,
\widetilde{A}_{j}^{\circ}\circ\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\circ}
is the identity on
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
, and
\widetilde{B}_{j}^{\circ}=\uptau^{-1}\circ B_{j}^{\circ}\circ\uptau

by the above.

A weighted version of Proposition
5.6

We adopt the setting of the subsection
Damping factors
of Section
5
, and
make the same assumptions as in the paragraph before Proposition
5.6
.
Thus
H
,
K
,
A
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
a_{0}
are as in the previous subsection,

{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}}
satisfies
{\mathfrak{v}}(t)>0
for all
t\geqslant a_{0}
, and its germ
{\mathfrak{v}}
is in
H
with
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
.
As part of those assumptions we also have
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
in the asymptotic field
K
,
for the convex subgroup

\Delta\ :=\ \big\{\gamma\in v(H^{\times}):\ \gamma=o(v{\mathfrak{v}})\big\}

of
v(H^{\times})=v(K^{\times})
. Also
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
and
u:={\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}|_{[a,\infty)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\times}
.

To state a weighted version of Proposition
5.6
, let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
,
{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
, and let
{\mathfrak{m}}
also denote a representative in
(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\times}
of the germ
{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Set
\uptau:={\mathfrak{m}}|_{[a,\infty)}
, so we have
\uptau\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\times}\cap(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
and thus
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
(Note that
\uptau
, like
u
, depends on
a
, but we do not indicate this dependence notationally.)
With notations as in the previous subsection we assume that for all
a
we have the factorization

\widetilde{A}_{a}\ =\ \widetilde{A}_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{A}_{r}\ \colon\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag],

as can be arranged by increasing
a_{0}
if necessary.

Proposition 9.7
.

Assume
H
is real closed,
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\nu>r
,
and the elements
\phi_{j}
,
\phi_{j}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
of
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
are alike for
j=1,\dots,r
.
Then:

(i)

the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
uA_{a}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
maps
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
into
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
;

(ii)

its restriction to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear map
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
is continuous; and

(iii)

denoting this restriction also by
uA_{a}^{-1}
, we have
\|uA_{a}^{-1}\|^{\uptau}_{a;r}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, so
g:=\uptau^{-1}f\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Let
i\in\{0,\dots,r\}
; then with
\widetilde{B}^{\circ}_{j}
as in the previous subsection and
u_{i,j}
as in Lemma
5.5
, that lemma gives

\uptau^{-1}\big(uA_{a}^{-1}(f)\big){}^{(i)}=\sum_{j=r-i}^{r}u_{i,j}u\cdot\uptau^{-1}B^{\circ}_{j}(\uptau g)\ =\sum_{j=r-i}^{r}u_{i,j}u\widetilde{B}^{\circ}_{j}(g).

The proof of Proposition
5.6
shows
u_{i,j}u\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
with
\lVert u_{i,j}u\rVert_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
.
Set

\widetilde{c}_{i,a}\ :=\ \sum_{j=r-i}^{r}\|u\,u_{i,j}\|_{a}\cdot\|\widetilde{B}_{j}\|_{a}\cdots\|\widetilde{B}_{1}\|_{a}\in[0,\infty)\qquad(i=0,\dots,r).

Then

\big\|\uptau^{-1}\big[uA_{a}^{-1}(f)\big]{}^{(i)}\big\|_{a}\leqslant\widetilde{c}_{i,a}\|g\|_{a}=\widetilde{c}_{i,a}\|f\|^{\uptau}_{a}

where
\widetilde{c}_{i,a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
. This yields (i)–(iii).
∎

Weighted variants of results in Section
6

In this subsection we adopt the hypotheses in force for Lemma
6.1
. To summarize those,

H
,
K
,
A
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
a_{0}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}
,
\nu
,
u
,
\Delta
are as in the previous subsection,

d,r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
,
H
is real closed,
R\in K\{Y\}
has order
\leqslant d
and weight
\leqslant w\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant r}
.
Also
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\nu>w
,

R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
,
\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\not\sim\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}
and

\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\in(\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}})^{\times}

for
j=1,\dots,r
. Finally,
\widetilde{A}:=A_{\ltimes{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}}\in K[\der]
and
\widetilde{A}_{a}(y)=u^{-1}A_{a}(uy)
for
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]
.
Next, let
{\mathfrak{m}}
,
\uptau
be as in the previous subsection. As in Lemma
6.10
we consider the
continuous operator

\Phi_{a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\times\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}

given by

\Phi_{a}(f,y)\ :=\ \Xi_{a}(f+y)-\Xi_{a}(f)\ =\ u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\!\left(u^{-1}\big(R(f+y)-R(f)\big)\right).

Here is our weighted version of Lemma
6.10
:

Lemma 9.8
.

Suppose the elements
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
,
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
of
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
are alike, for
j=1,\dots,r
, and let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then the operator
y\mapsto\Phi_{a}(f,y)

maps
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
into itself. Moreover, there are
E_{a},E_{a}^{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\geqslant}
such that for all
g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
and
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
,

\lVert\Phi_{a}(f,y)\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau},

\|\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\|_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ \\
E_{a}^{+}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

We can take these
E_{a}
,
E_{a}^{+}
such that
E_{a},E_{a}^{+}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, and do so below.

Proof.

Let
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.
By Taylor expansion we have

R(f+y)-R(f)\ =\ \sum_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>0}\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{i}}!}R^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}(f)y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\ =\ \sum_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>0}S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f)y^{{\boldsymbol{i}}}\quad\text{where $S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f):=\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{i}}!}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({\boldsymbol{i}})}f^{\boldsymbol{j}}$,}

and
u^{-1}S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. So

h:=u^{-1}\big(R(f+y)-R(f)\big)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, since
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.
Applying Proposition
9.7
(i) with
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
in the role of
\phi_{j}
yields
\Phi_{a}(f,y)=u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(h)\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, establishing the first claim. Next, let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
. Then
\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)=\Phi_{a}(f+g,y)
by (
6.1
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\ =\ u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(h),

\displaystyle\quad h\ :=\ u^{-1}\big(R(f+g+y)-R(f+g)\big),\text{ so}

\displaystyle\lVert\Phi_{a}(f,g+y)-\Phi_{a}(f,g)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\

\displaystyle=\ \lVert u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}(h)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ \lVert u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\cdot\lVert h\rVert^{\uptau}_{a}.

By Corollary
9.6
we have

\lVert h\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ D\cdot\max_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>0}\,\lVert u^{-1}S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f+g)\rVert_{a}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}

where
D=D(d,r):=\big(\textstyle{d+r+1\choose r+1}-1\big)
.
Let
D_{a}
be as in the proof of Lemma
6.2
. Then
D_{a}\to 0
as
a\to\infty
, and Lemma
6.9
gives for
\lvert{\boldsymbol{i}}\rvert>0
,

\displaystyle\lVert u^{-1}S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f)\rVert_{a}\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ D_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d}\big\}

\displaystyle\lVert u^{-1}S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}}(f+g)\rVert_{a}\

\displaystyle\leqslant\ D_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f+g\rVert_{a;r}^{d}\big\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ 2^{d}D_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert f\rVert_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\lVert g\rVert_{a;r}^{d}\big\}.

This gives the desired result for

E_{a}:=\lVert u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\cdot D\cdot D_{a}
and
E_{a}^{+}:=2^{d}E_{a}
, using also Proposition
9.7
(iii)
with
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
in the role of
\phi_{j}
.
∎

Lemma
9.8
allows us to refine Theorem
6.3
as follows:

Corollary 9.9
.

Suppose the elements
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
,
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
of
\mathcal{C}_{a_{0}}[\imag]
are alike, for
j=1,\dots,r
, and
R(0)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then
for sufficiently large
a
the operator
\Xi_{a}
maps the closed ball

B_{a}:=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]:\,\|f\|_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

of the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
into itself, has a unique fixed point in
B_{a}
, and this fixed point
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.

Proof.

Take
a
such that
\lVert\uptau\rVert_{a}\leqslant 1
. Then by (
9.2
),
B_{a}
contains the closed ball

B_{a}^{\uptau}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]:\,\|f\|^{\uptau}_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

of the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. Let
f,g\in B_{a}^{\uptau}
. Then

\Xi_{a}(g)-\Xi_{a}(f)=\Phi_{a}(f,g-f)
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
by Lemma
9.8
, and
with
E_{a}
as in that lemma,

\displaystyle\lVert\Xi_{a}(f)-\Xi_{a}(g)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\

\displaystyle=\ \lVert\Phi_{a}(f,g-f)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ E_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\cdots+\lVert g-f\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert g-f\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ E_{a}\cdot d\cdot\lVert g-f\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}.

Taking
a
so that moreover
E_{a}d\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
we obtain

(9.4)

\lVert\Xi_{a}(f)-\Xi_{a}(g)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\leqslant\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert f-g\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\quad\text{ for all
$f,g\in B_{a}^{\uptau}$.}

Next we consider the case
g=0
.
Our hypothesis
R(0)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}}
gives
u^{-1}R(0)\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. Proposition
9.7
(i),(ii)
with
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
in the role of
\phi_{j}
gives
\Xi_{a}(0)\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
and
\lVert\Xi_{a}(0)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\leqslant\lVert u\widetilde{A}_{a}^{-1}\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\lVert u^{-1}R(0)\rVert_{a}^{\uptau}
.
Using Proposition
9.7
(iii) we now take
a
so large that

\lVert\Xi_{a}(0)\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
. Then (
9.4
) for
g=0
gives
\Xi_{a}(B_{a}^{\uptau})\subseteq B_{a}^{\uptau}
.
By Lemma
9.2
the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
is complete,
hence
\Xi_{a}
has a unique fixed point in
B_{a}^{\uptau}
.
∎

Now suppose in addition that
A\in H[\der]
and
R\in H\{Y\}
. Set

(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\uptau}\ :=\ \big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}:\lVert f\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}<\infty\}\ =\ \mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a},

a real Banach space with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}
.
Increase
a_{0}
as at the beginning of the subsection
Preserving reality
of Section
6
. Then we have the map

\operatorname{Re}\Phi_{a}\colon(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}\times(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}\to(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}},\qquad(f,y)\mapsto\operatorname{Re}\!\big(\Phi_{a}(f,y)\big).

Suppose the elements
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}
,
\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}-{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
are alike for
j=1,\dots,r
, and let
a
and
E_{a},E_{a}^{+}
be as in
Lemma
9.8
. Then this lemma yields:

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
f,g\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
and
y\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\uptau}
. Then
(\operatorname{Re}\Phi_{a})(f,y)\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\uptau}
and

\lVert\operatorname{Re}(\Phi_{a})(f,y)\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\ \leqslant\ E_{a}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau},

\|(\operatorname{Re}\Phi_{a})(f,g+y)-(\operatorname{Re}\Phi_{a})(f,g)\|_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ \\
E_{a}^{+}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|f\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

The same way we derived Corollary
9.9
from Lemma
9.8
, this leads to:

Corollary 9.11
.

If
R(0)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}}
, then
for sufficiently large
a
the operator
\operatorname{Re}\Xi_{a}
maps the closed ball

B_{a}:=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}:\,\|f\|_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\}

of the normed space
(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
into itself, has a unique fixed point in
B_{a}
, and this fixed point
lies in
(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\uptau}
.

Revisiting Lemma
6.11

We adopt the setting of the
previous subsection. As usual,
a
ranges over
[a_{0},\infty)
.
We continue the investigation of the differences
{f-g}
between solutions
f
,
g

of the equation (

∗

‣
6
) on
[a_{0},\infty)
that began in Lemma
6.4
, and so we
take
f
,

,
E
,
\varepsilon
,
h_{a}
,

as in that lemma.
Recall that in the remarks preceding Lemma
6.11
we defined continuous operators
\Phi_{a},\Psi_{a}\colon\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}\to\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
by

\Phi_{a}(y)\ :=\ \Phi_{a}(g,y)\ =\ \Xi_{a}(g+y)-\Xi_{a}(g),\quad\Psi_{a}(y)\ :=\ \Phi_{a}(y)+h_{a}\qquad(y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}).

As in those remarks, we set
\rho:=\lVert f-g\rVert_{a_{0};r}
and

B_{a}\ :=\ \big\{y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}:\ \lVert y-h_{a}\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant 1/2\big\},

and take

so that
\theta_{a}\in B_{a}
for all

. Then by (

) we have
\lVert y\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant 1+\rho

for

and

.
Next, take
a_{2}\geqslant a_{1}
as in Lemma
6.11
; thus
for

and

we have

\Psi_{a}(y)\in B_{a}
and
\lVert{\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)}\rVert_{a;r}\leqslant\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\|y-z\|_{a;r}
.
As in the previous subsection,
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
,
{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
denotes also a representative in
(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a_{0}})^{\times}
of the germ
{\mathfrak{m}}
, and
\uptau:={\mathfrak{m}}|_{[a,\infty)}\in(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\times}\cap(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a})^{\operatorname{b}}
, so
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
.

In the rest of this subsection

\phi_{1}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger},\dots,\phi_{r}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\in K
are
\gamma
-repulsive for
\gamma:=v{\mathfrak{m}}\in v(H^{\times})^{>}
,
and
h_{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
for all

.

Then Lemma
1.15

gives
a_{3}\geqslant a_{2}
such that for all

and

, the functions
\phi_{j}-u^{\dagger},\phi_{j}-(u\uptau)^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}[\imag]
are alike and hence
\Psi_{a}\big(\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}\big)\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}

by Lemma
9.8
.
Thus
\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
for all
n
and
a\geqslant a_{3}
.

For
a\geqslant a_{2}
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
by Corollary
6.12
;
we now aim to strengthen this to “in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
” (possibly for a larger
a_{2}
).
Towards this:

Lemma 9.12
.

There exists
a_{4}\geqslant a_{3}
such that

\lVert{\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)}\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|y-z\|^{\uptau}_{a;r}
for all
a\geqslant a_{4}
and
y,z\in B_{a}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.

Proof.

For
a\geqslant a_{3}
and
y,z\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, and with
E_{a}^{+}
as in Lemma
9.8
we have

\|\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)\|_{a;r}^{\uptau}\ \leqslant\ \\
E_{a}^{+}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\max\!\big\{1,\|z\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y-z\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y-z\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\cdot\lVert y-z\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}.

For each
a\geqslant a_{1}
and
y,z\in B_{a}
we then have

\max\!\big\{1,\|z\|_{a;r}^{d}\big\}\cdot\big(1+\lVert y-z\rVert_{a;r}+\cdots+\lVert y-z\rVert_{a;r}^{d-1}\big)\ \leqslant\ (1+\rho)^{d}\cdot d,

so taking
a_{4}\geqslant a_{3}
with

E_{a}^{+}\max\!\big\{1,\|g\|_{a_{0};r}^{d}\big\}(1+\rho)^{d}d\leqslant 1/2\quad\text{ for all $a\geqslant a_{4}$,}

we have
\lVert{\Psi_{a}(y)-\Psi_{a}(z)}\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|y-z\|^{\uptau}_{a;r}
for all
a\geqslant a_{4}
and
y,z\in B_{a}\cap\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
.
∎

Let
a_{4}
be as in the previous lemma.

Corollary 9.13
.

Suppose
a\geqslant a_{4}
. Then
\theta_{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
in the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
. In particular,
f-g,(f-g)^{\prime},\dots,(f-g)^{(r)}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

We have
\Phi_{a}(h_{a})=\Psi_{a}(h_{a})-h_{a}\in\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, so
M:=\lVert\Phi_{a}(h_{a})\rVert_{a;r}^{\uptau}<\infty
. Since
\Psi_{a}(B_{a})\subseteq B_{a}
,
induction on
n
using Lemma
9.12
gives

\lVert\Psi_{a}^{n+1}(h_{a})-\Psi^{n}_{a}(h_{a})\rVert^{\uptau}_{a;r}\leqslant M/2^{n}\qquad\text{ for all $n$.}

Thus
\big(\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})\big)
is a cauchy sequence in the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
, and so converges in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
by Lemma
9.2
.
In the normed space
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\operatorname{b}}
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
,
by Corollary
6.12
.
Thus
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi_{a}^{n}(h_{a})=\theta_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}^{r}_{a}[\imag]^{\uptau}
by Lemma
9.1
.
∎

An application to slots in
H

Here we adopt the setting of the subsection
An application to slots in
H
in Section
4
. Thus

H\supseteq{\mathbb{R}}
is a Liouville closed Hardy field,

K:=H[\imag]
,

\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, and

(P,1,\widehat{h})
is a slot in
H
of order
r\geqslant 1
; we set
w:=\operatorname{wt}(P)
,
d:=\deg P
.

Assume also that
K
is
1
-linearly surjective if
r\geqslant 3
.

Proposition 9.14
.

Suppose
(P,1,\widehat{h})
is special, ultimate,
Z
-minimal, deep, and strongly repulsive-normal.
Let
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
be such that

P(f)\ =\ P(g)\ =\ 0,\qquad f,g\ \prec\ 1,\qquad v{\mathfrak{m}}\in v(\widehat{h}-H).

Then
(f-g)^{(j)}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}
for
j=0,\dots,r
.

Proof.

We arrange
{{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1}
. Let
{\mathfrak{v}}:=\lvert{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{P})\rvert\in H^{>}
, so
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec^{\flat}1
, and set
\Delta:=\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})
.
Take
Q,R\in H\{Y\}
where
Q
is homogeneous of degree
1
and order
r
,

A:=L_{Q}\in H[\der]
has a strong
\widehat{h}
-repulsive splitting over
K
,

P=Q-R
, and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}P_{1}
, so
{\mathfrak{v}}(A)\sim{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{P})
by Lemma
1.5
. Multiplying
P
,
Q
,
R
by some
b\in H^{\times}
we
arrange that
A
is monic, so
A=\der^{r}+f_{1}\der^{r-1}+\cdots+f_{r}
with
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in H
and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
.
Let
(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r})\in K^{r}
be a strong
\widehat{h}
-repulsive splitting of
A
over
K
, so
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
are
\widehat{h}
-repulsive and

A\ =\ (\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}),\qquad\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\ \succcurlyeq\ {\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\ \succcurlyeq\ 1.

By (
1.3
) we have
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
. Thus we can take
a_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and functions on
[a_{0},\infty)
representing the germs
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
,
f
,
g
, as well as the
R_{{\boldsymbol{j}}}
with
{{\boldsymbol{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{1+r}}
,
|{\boldsymbol{j}}|\leqslant d
,
\|{\boldsymbol{j}}\|\leqslant w
(using the same symbols for the germs mentioned as for their chosen representatives)
so as to be in the situation described in the beginning of Section
6
, with
f
and
g
solutions on
[a_{0},\infty)
of the differential equation (

∗

‣
6
) there.
As there, we take
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\nu>w
so that
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
and
\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\not\sim\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}

for
j=1,\dots,r
, and then increase
a_{0}
to satisfy all assumptions for Lemma
6.1
.
Proposition
1.9
gives

v({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu})\in v(\widehat{h}-H)
, so

\phi_{j}-\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}=\phi_{j}-({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu})^{\dagger}
(
j=1,\dots,r
)
is
\widehat{h}
-repulsive by
[
8
, Lemma 4.5.13(iv)]
, so
\gamma
-repulsive for
\gamma:=v{\mathfrak{m}}>0
. Now
A
splits over
K
, and
K
is
1
-linearly surjective if
r\geqslant 3
, hence

\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
by (
1.2
) and Lemma
1.4
.
Thus
by Corollary
4.13
we have
y,y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
for all
y\in\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]
with
A(y)=0
,
y\prec 1
.
In particular,
{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}h_{a},{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}h_{a}^{\prime},\dots,{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}h_{a}^{(r)}\prec 1
for all
a\geqslant a_{0}
. Thus the assumptions on
{\mathfrak{m}}
and the
h_{a}
made just before Lemma
9.12
are satisfied for a suitable choice of
a_{2}
,
so we can appeal to Corollary
9.13
.
∎

The assumption that
K
is
1
-linearly surjective for
r\geqslant 3
was only used in the proof above to obtain
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
. So if
A
as in this proof satisfies
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
, then we can drop this assumption about
K
, also in the next corollary.

Corollary 9.15
.

Suppose
(P,1,\widehat{h})
,
f
,
g
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
are as in Proposition
9.14
. Then

f-g\in{\mathfrak{m}}\,\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}.

Proof.

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\succcurlyeq 1
, then Lemma
8.1
(ii) applied with
y=(f-g)/{\mathfrak{m}}
and
1/{\mathfrak{m}}
in place of
{\mathfrak{m}}
gives what we want.
Now assume
{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
.
Since
\widehat{h}
is special over
H
, Proposition
9.14

applies to
{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1}
in place of
{\mathfrak{m}}
, so
(f-g)^{(j)}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1}
for
j=0,\dots,r
. Now apply Lemma
9.3
to suitable representatives of
f-g
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
.
∎

Later in this section we use Proposition
9.14
and its Corollary
9.15
to
strengthen some results from Section
8
.

Weighted refinements of results in Section
8

We now adopt the setting of the subsection
Reformulations
of Section
8
. Thus
H\supseteq{\mathbb{R}}
is a real closed Hardy field
with asymptotic integration, and
K:=H[\imag]\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
is its algebraic closure,
with value group
\Gamma:=v(H^{\times})=v(K^{\times})
.
The next lemma and its corollary refine Lemma
8.5
. Let
P
,
Q
,
R
,
L_{Q}
,
w
be as introduced before that lemma, set
{\mathfrak{v}}:=\lvert{\mathfrak{v}}(L_{Q})\rvert\in H^{>}
, and, in case
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
,
\Delta:=\Delta({\mathfrak{v}})
.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
f\in K^{\times}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\in K
be such that

L_{Q}\ =\ f(\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}),\qquad\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\ \succcurlyeq\ 1.

Assume
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}Q
. Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
,

{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
,
P(0)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+2}{\mathfrak{m}}Q
. Suppose that for
j=1,\dots,r
and all
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
w<\nu<w+1
,

\phi_{j}-({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu})^{\dagger}
and
{\phi_{j}-({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}})^{\dagger}}
are alike.
Then
P(y)=0
and
y,y^{\prime},\dots,y^{(r)}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}
for some
y\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
. If
P,Q\in H\{Y\}
,
then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
.

Proof.

Note that
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}
by (
1.3
), and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}Q
gives
f^{-1}R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
.
Take
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
w<\nu<w+1
,
f^{-1}R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}
and
\nu{\mathfrak{v}}^{\dagger}\not\sim\operatorname{Re}\phi_{j}

for
j=1,\dots,r
. Set
A\ :=\ f^{-1}L_{Q}
. From
\nu<w+1
and

R(0)\ =\ -P(0)\ \prec_{\Delta}\ {\mathfrak{v}}^{w+2}{\mathfrak{m}}Q

we obtain
f^{-1}R(0)\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}}
. Now apply successively Corollary
9.9
,
Lemma
6.1
, and
Corollary
7.5
to the equation
A(y)=f^{-1}R(y)
,
y\prec 1
in the role of (

∗

‣
6
) in Section
6
to obtain the first part. For the real variant, use instead Corollary
9.11
and
Lemma
6.5
.
∎

Lemma
9.16
with
{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1}
for
{\mathfrak{m}}
has the following consequence, using Lemma
9.3
:

Corollary 9.17
.

Let
f\in K^{\times}
and
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{r}\in K
be such that

L_{Q}\ =\ f(\der-\phi_{1})\cdots(\der-\phi_{r}),\qquad\operatorname{Re}\phi_{1},\dots,\operatorname{Re}\phi_{r}\ \succcurlyeq\ 1.

Assume
{\mathfrak{v}}\prec 1
and
R\prec_{\Delta}{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+1}Q
. Let
{\mathfrak{m}}\in H^{\times}
,

{\mathfrak{m}}\prec 1
,
P(0)\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{v}}^{w+2}{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1}Q
. Suppose
that for
{j=1,\dots,r}
and all
\nu\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
w<\nu<w+1
,

\phi_{j}-({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu})^{\dagger}
and
{\phi_{j}-({\mathfrak{v}}^{\nu}{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1})^{\dagger}}
are alike.
Then for some
y\prec{\mathfrak{v}}^{w}
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
we have
{P(y)=0}
and
y\in{\mathfrak{m}}\,\mathcal{C}^{r}[\imag]^{\preccurlyeq}
.
If
P,Q\in H\{Y\}
,
then there is such
y
in
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
.

Remark
.

If
H
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-Hardy field, then Lemma
9.16
and Corollary
9.17
go through with
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}[\imag]
,
\mathcal{C}^{<\infty}
replaced by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}[\imag]
,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, respectively. Likewise with
\mathcal{C}^{\omega}
in place of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
. (Use Corollary
7.5
.)

Next a variant of Lemma
8.6
.
In the rest of this subsection
(P,{\mathfrak{n}},\widehat{h})
is a deep, strongly repulsive-normal,
Z
-minimal slot in
H
of order
r\geqslant 1
and weight
w:=\operatorname{wt}(P)
. We assume also that
